1,2026-10-24,"When Hans Romine finished his architecture degree in 2025, the 24-year-old began looking for work. After about 50 job applications yielded a single interview appointment, he started feeling despondent.
That's when a friend told him about a new show called 877UNMPLYD.
The show is on Twitch, a streaming platform favored by gamers and fans, but has an unlikely host: Jeremy Zimmer, the former CEO of United Talent Agency, along with digital-savvy producer Rachel Braun. The show offers career advice, discusses labor market news, and fields live call-ins from people looking for work.
When Romine called in, he mentioned he'd spent a few months as a recreation supervisor, building morale at an isolated research station in Antarctica. Zimmer and Braun found that the most interesting part of his whole story, and told him to play it up in applications and interviews.
Romine took their advice, and when he went in for that interview, he highlighted his experiences out on the tundra. After he landed the job, he called back in to let the hosts know.
That's the kind of win Zimmer and Braun hope to replicate. Over their first season, the duo hosted 12 episodes, with one episode filmed live on the streets of New York garnering over 54,000 views.
The show is a small experiment in career guidance at a moment when the entry-level job market is getting harder to navigate — especially in New York City. A new report from Partnership for New York City, a nonprofit business advocacy group, found that the number of entry-level jobs has shrunk disproportionately among professions highly exposed to AI; entry-level postings in design, media, and writing fell by 40.6%.
Nationally, employment in white-collar industries has fallen, even as output has gone up — indicating that AI might be effectively replacing entry-level white-collar work.
That dearth of entry-level opportunities is part of what spawned the show, a partnership between TikTok savant Adam Faze and the economic mobility nonprofit Schultz Family Foundation. Judith Dubin, the head of content at the foundation, said that they'd drilled down on investments in career navigation after recognizing how difficult it is to be graduating from high school or college into the current labor market.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
""The current job marketplace is so disrupted with the advent of AI,"" Dubin said. ""It's just an additional barrier for young people.""
In August, Zimmer and Braun set up a desk on a plaza at Baruch College, with a sign that said ""Ask a CEO,"" and fielded questions from students. One student asked for advice on how to negotiate salary expectations, especially in the interview process. Braun said that not all advice-seekers are new grads: One Gen X marketer called in after being laid off, feeling pushed out of the tech industry.
Going into the show, Zimmer focused on what it must feel like when getting a job is no longer as simple as putting your head down, working hard, and expecting things to work out.
""That age-old mantra doesn't feel as relevant today,"" Zimmer said. He's been struck by the contrast between callers who are hyper-focused on what they want, eschewing any concern about why that can't happen, and those who are baffled that they've applied for hundreds of jobs and haven't gotten any interviews. He's found both the naivety and the determination of the callers exciting.
Braun, the show's other host and a producer, said that AI emerged as a major theme throughout their first season — both the opportunities it presented and the way that it was shaking up the entry-level job hunt.
Romine, who landed a job, hopes that employers can once again understand the value of having fresh talent on their teams. But as the labor market continues its entry-level contraction, getting a leg up — even if it's through advice on a Twitch show — is ever more important.
""In the meantime, students and graduates need to really figure out how to work the current system and make themselves stand out in such an intense market,"" Romine said.

2,2026-10-24,"Contents
23 October 2026
·
11 min read
·AI, Quantitative Finance
Timestral is Mistral’s new family of models for forecasting time series. Give it a sequence of numbers, such as daily sales or electricity demand, and it predicts what comes next. It also produces a range of possible values, so we can ask how uncertain the forecast is.
The family runs from 5 million to 209 million parameters and supports multiple related series and extra inputs. We were interested in the 28-million-parameter version: small enough to run locally, and already trained across other time series before we gave it our market data.
We tested Timestral on daily stock returns, trading volume and a measure of volatility, then fine-tuned the model on older market data. The whole experiment ran on a 16 GB M2 MacBook Air, without a GPU.
Volume gave us the clearest result. In our 2026 research note, the original Timestral-28M reduced fifth-session stock log-volume error by 4.70% against a conditional regression. Fine-tuning moved that to 4.94%, but the direct comparison gave us little reason to prefer the adapted model. Returns were much less encouraging.
The six charts below expand the single figure in that three-page note.
We downloaded daily market data from Yahoo from 2010 onward. The main panel contained fourteen U.S. stocks, including Apple, Microsoft, JPMorgan, Exxon and Coca-Cola, plus three equity funds: SPY, QQQ and IWM. We kept three other funds out of fine-tuning and model selection to test transfer later.
At each forecast date, the model received the preceding 512 completed daily observations. We scored its prediction for the next trading session and for the fifth session ahead.
A fifth-session return means the daily return on that future day, rather than the cumulative return over the next five days.
We used logarithms for volume because trading activity can vary enormously across companies and dates. They also stop the largest volume spikes from dominating the error calculation. For each asset, we compared the model’s average absolute error with the baseline’s, then gave every asset equal weight.
A 4.70% improvement here means a reduction in that log-volume error measure. It does not mean a 4.70% investment return, or even a 4.70% reduction in error measured in shares.
The volatility target uses the Parkinson high–low range estimator. It captures how far prices moved within the daily range. It leaves out overnight jumps and is different from volatility calculated from intraday returns.
We began by running the original model without further training, usually called zero-shot forecasting. Then we asked whether adapting it to recent financial history would help.
For each test year, training used five older years of labels. The following year was reserved for choosing among saved model versions, called checkpoints. Only after that did we score the forecasts on the test year.
We tested 150 forecast dates across 2023–2025, spaced five trading sessions apart. A separate 2026 snapshot gave us forty dates from 2 January to 13 October. The final fifth-session outcome fell on 20 October.
Each training run started again from the same original weights. We updated all 27.8 million parameters for 500 steps and compared checkpoints at steps 0, 100, 250 and 500 using the earlier validation year. The original model at step zero was allowed to win. It did so in three of the 27 historical selections.
We repeated this for three targets, four test years and three training-order seeds, giving 36 runs. For each target and year, we combined the three selected forecasts by taking their median at each predicted probability level. The seeds changed the order of training examples; they did not give us three new market histories.
Every fourth update trained on groups of related series, although the forecasts reported here used one series at a time. We kept one training recipe rather than searching many settings until something looked good.
The uncertainty bars in the charts come from resampling blocks of forecast dates, keeping assets together on each date. They are descriptive 95% intervals from 1,000 resamples. They do not repeat training or adjust for the many comparisons. They help show how stable a result is across the saved dates; a bar crossing zero includes both an improvement and a deterioration.
Across fourteen stocks in our retrospective 2026 evaluation, the original Timestral-28M reduced fifth-session log-volume error by 4.70% against a conditional regression. The descriptive 95% interval ran from 2.08% to 6.98%.
The fine-tuned Timestral-28M ensemble reduced the same error by 4.94%, with an interval from 2.76% to 7.11%. The two estimates were close enough that the chart nearly puts them on top of each other.
Fine-tuning had helped more in the historical sample. For the next session, the adapted model reduced error by 1.08% against the original, with an interval from 0.49% to 1.69%. That gain did not clearly carry into 2026.
Before building a training pipeline, we would first check how far the downloaded weights get us.
Using yesterday’s volume as tomorrow’s forecast is cheap, but it is not the only cheap forecast available.
Our conditional regression used the last log-volume observation, trailing five- and 22-session averages, and a known five-session lag where it added distinct information. It fitted only completed historical pairs inside the same 512-observation window. We added the median past fitted residual to its point forecast.
In our 2026 stock-volume comparison, the original Timestral-28M reduced fifth-session log-volume error by 18.58% against the last observed volume. The reduction was 4.70% against conditional regression. Against the past 20-session median, it was 3.20%, with an interval that crossed zero.
An 18% improvement sounds much more impressive than 5%. Both came from the same forecasts. The difference was what we asked the model to beat.
We added the regression after seeing the early historical volume results, then froze its specification before looking at the adapted-volume and 2026 scores. The older comparisons helped develop the experiment. The reserved period gave us a separate local check of those choices.
Timestral beat our regression on this stock-volume comparison. We did not establish that it beats every simple volume rule.
A model can beat a regression while learning almost nothing useful from our additional training. To separate those questions, we compared each task-adapted model directly with the original checkpoint.
In our 2026 evaluation, all twelve error comparisons between fine-tuned Timestral-28M and the original model had 95% intervals spanning zero. They covered three tasks, two forecast offsets, and the stock and equity-fund panels.
For fifth-session stock volume, the direct improvement was 0.14%, with an interval from a 1.15% deterioration to a 1.48% improvement. We would use that comparison to decide whether to maintain fine-tuned weights.
Subtracting the two headline regression scores would give a different number. Each score first divides an asset’s error by its comparator’s error. Changing the comparator changes that denominator, so the direct comparison must be calculated separately.
All nine 2026 checkpoint selections preferred a trained candidate on the earlier validation data. That preference still did not produce a clear improvement on the later test. Validation selected what looked best in 2025; it could not guarantee that the advantage would last.
We would therefore start a follow-up with the original 28M model. Fine-tuning would need to earn its place through a repeatable gain large enough to matter for the intended use.
Daily returns were harder to improve. Across the study, we found no consistent advantage over simple return forecasts. In the 2026 stock panel, the adapted fifth-session forecast was 0.38% worse than the past median.
A pretrained model can pick up useful regularities without turning daily price moves into an easy prediction problem. Other financial evaluations of time-series foundation models have also found that favourable model rankings can coexist with small and scattered gains over a random walk.
Range volatility was more mixed. The adapted stock forecast beat our HAR-style regression by 2.26% at the fifth-session offset in 2026. The original model already had approximately the same point estimate. For the three equity funds, the adapted model was 5.08% worse than that regression.
The HAR approach combines information over daily, weekly and monthly periods. Our version applied that idea to the daily range proxy. It outperformed the adapted model on the equity-fund range forecasts in 2026.
We did not convert these forecasts into a trading strategy. There were no simulated fills, transaction costs or profit estimates. Better volume forecasts might eventually help plan execution or allocate capacity, but those uses need their own test.
Timestral predicts quantiles as well as a central forecast. That lets us check whether its uncertainty ranges contain outcomes as often as advertised.
A nominal 90% interval should contain roughly nine out of ten observations. In our 2026 stock-return sample, the fine-tuned Timestral-28M’s nominal 90% intervals contained 83.75% of next-session outcomes and 84.11% of fifth-session outcomes. The original model covered 85.18% and 84.82% respectively.
The 50% and 80% intervals also fell below their nominal coverage. A forecast can have a reasonable central estimate while understating how often an unusual outcome will occur.
We would check and recalibrate these intervals before using them to set risk limits. They describe individual future observations, too. Adding daily quantiles together does not produce a valid uncertainty band for the cumulative price path.
We kept TLT, GLD and USO out of adaptation and checkpoint selection. They gave us a way to ask whether a model adapted to equities would carry over to other exposures: a Treasury fund, a gold fund and an oil-futures fund.
For fifth-session volume in 2026, neither the original nor the adapted model clearly beat conditional regression on any of those funds. Their intervals all crossed zero. The same was true for the three equity ETFs.
Some individual estimates were positive. TLT’s original model showed a 4.75% improvement, but its interval ran from a 5.01% deterioration to a 15.03% improvement. That is a wide range for deciding whether to rely on a forecast.
USO also represents an oil-futures fund, not a direct forecast of spot crude. We would test oil-market forecasts separately before drawing conclusions about that market.
All 36 Timestral-28M fine-tuning runs used 101.6 minutes of CPU training and validation on a 16 GB M2 MacBook Air. We used two threads and 32-bit floating-point arithmetic.
The elapsed interval from the first training start to completion of the queued work was 118.1 minutes, including preparation, gaps and overlapping scoring. Initial setup, earlier experiments and final audits took additional time.
We had also tried the 123M model in the initial zero-shot benchmark. That run took 592.3 seconds, compared with 201.8 seconds for 28M, while giving comparable volume gains. Those timings belonged to that first benchmark, rather than a general inference-speed test. They gave us a practical reason to concentrate the adaptation study on 28M.
Keeping the experiment local made it easier to preserve the actual models and forecasts. The research archive includes the downloaded data, original weights, selected checkpoints, frozen protocols, dependencies and verification scripts. After moving it to its own repository, we replayed the chronology and scores with reads from the original workspace blocked.
The charts here read the preserved result tables. They do not refit the models or rerun statistical tests. Their underlying rows and file hashes travel with the article.
We held the 2026 outcomes back from our local evaluation, but every forecast date preceded Timestral’s 15 October release. This was a retrospective test. We do not know which financial data the original model saw during pretraining, so our chronological fine-tuning split cannot establish a completely unseen test for the pretrained model. That problem is broader than this project, as research on foundation-model evaluation explains.
The panel is also a fixed selection of surviving stocks, using retrospectively adjusted data, with only forty reserved forecast dates.
For that next experiment, we would freeze the original 28M model, the volume comparators and the scoring rules before collecting post-release observations. We would then choose one concrete use for the volume forecasts and measure whether the error reduction improves that decision. The extra training can wait until it shows a reliable advantage over the model we can already download.
Topics: Timestral financial forecasting, Timestral stock volume forecasting, Timestral fine-tuning, Timestral CPU inference, time-series foundation models financial forecasting

4,2026-10-24,"Peter Thiel, evidently, isn't a fan of the iPhone Duo.
In fact, the billionaire PayPal cofounder and tech investor said he hasn't been very impressed with much of anything that Apple has done since its eccentric founder, Steve Jobs, died of cancer in 2011.
""In my telling, it did nothing new after Jobs,"" Thiel said of the iPhone maker in a September interview with author Walter Isaacson for the ""Builder's Podcast,"" which was released on Thursday.
""It turned out, yeah, the mobile phone was this giant invention — there was not that much more to do,"" he said. ""Then it was just scaling and branding and building it out over 15 years or whatever.""
Indeed, Apple has faced criticism for failing to innovate beyond the mobile phone and tablet, especially in the age of AI, where its ""Siri"" product is not as advanced as rivals from ChatGPT or Anthropic.
Jobs unveiled the first iPhone back in 2007 at a conference in San Francisco. Nearly 20 years later, Apple released the 18th iteration this week, with one slight caveat: This one folds.
That said, MacBooks are the de facto laptops for many an AI-nerd and normie alike. iPhones are ubiquitous, and the sleek AirPods design took hold when they were released in 2016.
Though Thiel told Maureen Dowd of The New York Times in 2017 that Apple's era was over, the company's stock price has grown steadily since then.
Thiel didn't pooh-pooh Apple entirely. He called it a ""pure, well-run value stock,"" but lamented what he described as the loss of vision after Jobs' death.

Apple didn't immediately respond to a request for comment sent on Saturday.

5,2026-10-24,"Ukraine's military uses the network of 10,000 low Earth orbit satellites extensively for military operations
Ukraine now has a ""stable connection"" to Starlink, its defence minister has said, after its military suffered days of restricted connectivity on the satellite internet network.
Kyiv relies on the Starlink and its military-grade counterpart Starshield for piloting drones and operations within Ukraine, but last week accused US President Donald Trump of telling owner Elon Musk to place limits on it. 
This meant drones would lose connection when travelling at 161km/h (100mph) or more, it said. Trump denied doing so before saying Musk had ""put it back"".
Defence Minister Yevhenii Khmara said that speeds of more than 400km/h were now possible on the network, indicating that full capability had been restored.
""This result is above all important for our front, for our soldiers, for the operation of drones, for logistics and for communications,"" he said in a statement on Saturday.
Ukraine's military uses the network of 10,000 low Earth orbit satellites extensively for military operations, particularly in remote areas on the battlefield. 
Ukraine uses both the civilian service Starlink and the more secure Starshield, which Musk said was controlled by the US military when he suggested the Pentagon could be behind the recent issues.
Russia is not authorised to use Starlink, so when Ukraine sends long-range missiles or drones deep into Russia to hit oil refineries, the network does not work.
The debacle over Starlink began last week, and emerged during a broader diplomatic row between Trump and his Ukrainian counterpart Volodymyr Zelensky over Ukrainian strikes on Russian oil infrastructure.
Faced with midterm elections next month, Trump has waived some sanctions on Russia and agreed a deal to import Russian diesel for the US market - a move criticised by Ukraine and its European allies.
Trump said the strikes on Russian oil refineries, which Kyiv says are crucial to stemming the main revenue source for Moscow's war effort, have pushed global prices higher.
While the attacks have had an impact on global fuel prices, rises seen this year have primarily been due to the Iran war.
Zelensky said that, as well as seemingly limiting Starlink capability in Ukraine, the US had also threatened to withdraw intelligence sharing if the strikes did not stop, something Trump also denied.
Meanwhile, fighting on the ground continues.
Russia's ministry of defence wrote on Telegram on Saturday that their air defences shot down 616 Ukrainian fixed-wing drones, striking a number of regions across the country, including Russian-annexed Crimea.
Sergey Aksyonov, the region's Moscow-backed governor, said restrictions on electricity and water had been put in place because of power cuts caused by the drone strike.
Meanwhile, Ukrainian authorities said they struck a missile component manufacturer, a chemical plant and an aircraft at a military airfield inside Russia in the past 24 hours.
They also said that Russia carried out 88 air strikes using 309 glide bombs and 10,270 kamikaze drones on Friday.

Tech > Mobile > Phones
Oppo's ambitious plans for the Find X10 Pro and its future high-end phones are America's loss but Europe's gain.
Principal Writer
Principal Writer
October 24, 2026, 5:02 am ET
If the only two names that pop into your head when you think about the best phones on the market are Apple and Samsung, frankly, you’re thinking too small.
Chinese phone-makers aren’t just making phones that rival those from Apple and Samsung; in some respects, they’re surpassing the iPhones and Galaxys — in batteries and fast charging, and especially when it comes to cameras.
On Wednesday, Oppo launched its latest photography-focused high-end phones, the Find X10 and Find X10 Pro, on the sun-bleached Spanish island of Mallorca. A finca covered in bougainvillea and surrounded by gnarled olive groves and roving wild goats might at first glance seem like an offbeat choice for a Chinese tech giant to stage a global launch event, but the decision is far from random. Spain is Oppo’s biggest European market, and it’s only getting bigger.
More from CNET
It’s a story we’re seeing repeat itself around the world. According to researchers Counterpoint and Omdia, Oppo was the fourth-largest phone-maker globally by shipments in the first half of this year. Already well established in China, the company is now seeing growth across the globe, and in Europe in particular.
It’s an impressive feat given that growing a smartphone business is harder than it’s ever been. It’s no secret that RAMageddon, the ongoing memory shortage, is pushing up phone prices and causing a massive drop in shipments. 
Phone-makers might be putting on a brave face as they release new devices to the world, but behind the scenes they’re scrambling to draw up new strategies that will hopefully see them through in both the short and long term.
Growing sales of flagship and premium phones will be key to their survival, said Runar Bjorhovde, principal analyst at market research firm Omdia, during a briefing at the Oppo event. Compared with the budget and midrange segments, the high-end segment is relatively resilient.
You might think that means more exciting (if expensive) phones everywhere – witness the launch of Apple’s folding iPhone Duo, which starts at $1,999 – particularly in wealthy countries such as the US. But that’s not exactly the case.
When it comes to premium phone markets, none is larger or more profitable than the US. And yet Oppo and other Chinese smartphone-makers are steering well clear.
There are several reasons for this. Firstly, they don’t have Apple’s and Samsung’s long-standing carrier relationships that would facilitate their phones being presented as solid options to buyers. Together with the geopolitical roadblocks affecting Chinese tech companies, this makes the US simply too tricky and precarious right now to make that investment worthwhile. 
“The barriers to enter make it almost impossible to go in and actually scale there unless you have lots of money to invest,” said Bjorhovde. Instead, the money they do have to invest is being poured into Europe. (Oppo said it will increase its investment in its European business by 30% over the next three years.)
This is bad news for Americans, who simply don’t and won’t be able to benefit from the full array of choices on offer to people in Europe and beyond, including the Find X10 Pro. In CNET’s review of the device, Editor at Large Andrew Lanxon described it as “a powerhouse phone that’s well worthy of its flagship title,” praising its cameras as the best on the market alongside the iPhone 18 Pro and Xiaomi’s Leitzphone.
Of these three, only one is on sale in the US, but Europeans can take their pick.
“It definitely feels like US consumers are missing out by not having the latest and greatest smartphones from the leading Chinese manufacturers,” said Ben Wood, chief analyst at FDM CCS. It’s especially noticeable when it comes to some of the cutting-edge foldable phones from companies like Honor, Oppo and Xiaomi, he added.
The Apple-Samsung duopoly that dominates the American market also exists in Europe, albeit to a lesser extent. But Elvis Zhou, Oppo’s European CEO, wants to see it broken completely.
“People deserve more exciting options,” he said in a briefing with journalists. When it comes to picking a premium phone, he thinks people should have a choice between three or four brands, rather than just two.
This reflects the more diverse array of successful phone brands in China, he added. In his company’s home country, there are only a few percentage points difference between Huawei, Apple, Oppo, Vivo, Xiaomi and Honor.
Xiaomi, Honor and Vivo are all now vying with Oppo for a slice of the European pie. One sign of intensifying competition is the shrinking gap between products being launched in China and Europe, which has historically spanned months. Zhou says that’s changing — the Find X10 series launched in China only a month ago.
When it comes to choosing a premium phone, Omdia’s research shows that buyers place more importance on camera quality than do people buying budget and midrange devices, but also “softer” values, including preferred brand, perceived status and aesthetics. Oppo has nailed its camera system, but the same isn’t necessarily true of the latter values.
Zhou isn’t worried, though. Oppo is squarely targeting Gen Z with the Find X10 series (Omdia research shows that people under 35 are most likely to buy premium phones), and he thinks they’re savvy enough to look beyond ads to first-person recommendations and chatbots that will provide specs-based advice to discern which product will offer the best value.
“We want to focus on the young generation because … they know how to make the decision very logically – they will not make the decision only following the brand name,” he said. “Young people really care about the product itself, and they can get all the information they want from AI and from social media. It’s a good opportunity for me.”
The company has already found success using this strategy for the Find X9 series, which saw its share of the premium European market quadruple between 2025 and 2026, according to Omdia.
But while Oppo might have made headway in Europe, it might be difficult to sustain this effort over time, said Wood — especially given that all of its Chinese rivals are adopting a very similar strategy at the very same moment.
Competition might be fierce, but Oppo is ready to play the long game. “We’re very patient,” Zhou said with a wry smile. “We’re not rushing here in Europe.”
Editors’ note: Katie Collins’ travel costs were covered by Oppo. The judgments and opinions of CNET are our own.

7,2026-10-24,"ReadWrite's editorial staff consists of journalists, editors, and experts across iGaming & Technology. Over 20 years of combined experience and the latest in technology, AI,…
The Commodity Futures Trading Commission has told prediction-market operators Novig and ProphetX not to list a set of sports event contracts the two companies had already self-certified, according to Front Office Sports. The agency also asked both firms to explain how the markets would comply with federal commodities law.
A CFTC spokesperson confirmed that letters had been sent to both companies. “Any derivatives contract offered by a CFTC-regulated exchange must be compliant with the Commodity Exchange Act,” the spokesperson said.
The two letters target different products. Novig was told that its micro contracts, such as a market on which player opens the scoring in a basketball game, are open to manipulation. ProphetX was told that its scorigami contracts, which settle on whether a game finishes with a scoreline that has never occurred in that sport, do not meet the Commodity Exchange Act’s definition of a swap.
Sources told Front Office Sports that the letters describe the filings as false self-certifications, the term used when an exchange certifies a contract that may not be permitted under the statute. Neither set of contracts had been listed, so no customer has traded on them.
ProphetX framed the request more narrowly. A company spokesperson said CFTC staff “requested that we voluntarily defer listing the Scorigami contracts pending further review, not that we withdraw them.” The company said it complied, has since supplied further analysis on the contracts’ compliance and economic utility, and remains in contact with agency staff. Novig did not immediately respond to a request for comment, the outlet reported.
Under the current framework, exchanges can self-certify and list new contracts without prior approval, and the CFTC can step in afterward. The agency would not say if it has ever before told an operator to hold back a self-certified sports contract. One source described the letters as “kind of a warning shot” for the sector.
The move does not appear to be limited to two companies. Senior CFTC officials have also spoken with DraftKings and FanDuel and cautioned that listing micro contracts could bring the same response, according to the report. Other exchanges have voiced frustration with the agency’s stance, pointing out that many state regulators allow comparable in-game wagers at licensed sportsbooks.
The letters follow a separate round of CFTC inquiries last month into incentive programs at Kalshi, Polymarket, Rothera and Novig. They also come ahead of a final rule the agency is expected to issue after its June proposal, a 267-page notice that would restrict in-game props and limit markets on high school sports. The proposal sits inside a wider dispute over how sports contracts are treated under federal rules, with several states arguing those products should fall under gambling law.
One source said the Novig and ProphetX markets were exactly the type of event contracts professional leagues have objected to most. On October 19, the NBA tightened the rules on player props and micro bets for its sportsbook partners, among them markets on who scores a game’s first basket, Yogonet reported. Those league rules cover traditional sportsbooks, not prediction markets.
It is not clear what the CFTC would do if either company went ahead and listed the contracts. Both firms have the chance to make their case to the agency, and the outcome is likely to shape how far other exchanges push into single-play sports markets before the final rule lands. Sports already account for most of the activity on the largest platforms, as shown in recent data on NFL trading volume across prediction markets.
The ReadWrite Editorial policy involves closely monitoring the gambling and blockchain industries for major developments, new product and brand launches, game releases and other newsworthy events. 8,2026-10-24,"American parking lots are awash with a sea of sameness. There's just a bunch of basic SUVs and pickup trucks.
That makes the wagon-ish 2026 Subaru Trailseeker EV a real eye-catcher. Unlike the homogeneous crossovers rolling up to elementary school pickup lines today, it gets a stretched, long-roof profile and carries a Subaru badge. It looks like a proper successor to the Subaru wagons of yore, like the 2000s Outbacks and Legacys.
Technically, Subaru sees this differently. They classify the Trailseeker as an all-electric midsize SUV — and they do have a point with its 8.5 inches of ground clearance and easy-to-slide-into seats.
But, I mean, come on, look at this thing.
That's a wagon in my book.
I drove the Trailseeker's top Touring trim for about 450 miles to see if it was a worthy antidote to America's great car-design flattening. My tester — complete with premium blue paint, a panoramic glass roof, and radiant leg warmers in the front seat (yes, you read that right) — stickered at $48,400.
And it was pretty wild.
I've driven around 80 cars in my career as an auto journalist: EVs, muscle cars, sports cars, big trucks, premium luxury vehicles, etc.
The Trailseeker easily makes my personal list of top 10 fastest vehicles. It can launch from standing to 60 mph in about 4.4 seconds and produces 375 horsepower.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
Get behind the steering wheel of this family-wagon-shaped car, and you feel that power at all times. On my highway-dominant drive from New York City to the Washington, DC, area, it quickly dashed by slower traffic and 18-wheelers.
The speed doesn't come at the expense of efficiency or refinement, either.
The Trailseeker returned about 3.7 miles per kWh, giving the battery about 270 miles of juice. While it was whipping past cars on the New Jersey Turnpike, I could still hear back-seat passengers when they spoke.
And, speaking of the back seat, the Trailseeker's long body gives rear passengers an almost comical amount of legroom. Fold those seats down, and my panoramic-roof tester has up to 71.8 cubic feet of cargo space, in case you have to move some furniture around, too.
Its biggest red flag was charging.
I saw speeds peak around 127 kW, which is pretty middling in today's EV age. Subaru says the car can accept up to 150 kW.
Let's compare that to the much higher-priced (but similarly shaped) Lucid Gravity, which is considered America's fastest-charging EV. That one can accept up to 420 kW, adding up to 200 miles of range in under 12 minutes.
My initial fast-charge of the Trailseeker — a top-off kind of stop while I grabbed a coffee — took 21 minutes, charging the car from 49% to 77%.
Subaru didn't build the Trailseeker alone.
It's the product of Subaru's expanding EV partnership with Toyota. The two companies now sell three closely related EV pairs: Subaru's Solterra, Uncharted, and Trailseeker correspond with Toyota's bZ, C-HR, and bZ Woodland. Next year, the companies are launching mechanically similar three-row EVs, too, with the Toyota Highlander and the Subaru Getaway.
The Trailseeker is easily the Subaru-iest of the bunch, and it's not just because of its wagon-y looks. It gets a dose of Subaru's adventure-seeking vibe, including standard Symmetrical All-Wheel Drive, X-Mode settings for snow and mud, roof rails, and a 3,500-pound towing capacity.
That outdoorsy tuning comes with a trade-off. The Trailseeker felt noticeably firm over broken pavement, especially at lower speeds.
You want a wagon now, right? Isn't this thing so cool?
Well, I'm teasing you. Good luck finding a Trailseeker.
Right now, Subaru has only a 9.7-day supply of the model in dealer lots, per CarGurus data shared with Business Insider. That's well under the industry average of about 70 days' worth of supply.
Kevin Roberts, CarGurus's director of economic and market intelligence, says the low supply isn't a product of high demand. In September, the automaker only sold 849 units, just 1.6% of all Subaru sales in America.
Rather, the low supply is part of an ongoing trend of automakers slowly shipping 2027 model-year vehicles to lots.
As the calendar turns, Roberts says there's little momentum for the wagon body type: ""This segment has accounted for less than 1% of new car sales,"" he said, adding that ""the traditional wagon has become increasingly rare in today's new car market.""
So, no, the Trailseeker probably isn't about to resurrect the American station wagon.
That's a shame. After 450 miles in one, I'm increasingly convinced that good ones could win over American drivers.

9,2026-10-24,"The fight between Android and iPhone has been long and arduous, but one company has learned how to play both sides. Along with having its own lineup of impressive smartphones that compete with Apple, South Korean manufacturer Samsung is a rather dominating force when it comes to display panels in general. And that may be why the company stands to potentially make more from iPhones than even its own Galaxy devices.
If you've been wondering who makes the iPhone Duo's screen, the short answer is Samsung. The company has a three-year agreement to be the sole supplier of Apple's OLED displays for the foldable. Additionally, Samsung is also supplying OLED panels for the iPhone 18 Pro and iPhone 18 Pro Max, though LG is also providing displays for those. Reports suggest that panels in the iPhone Duo could net Samsung hundreds of dollars per device.
This also wouldn't be the first time Samsung stood to win big off Apple, as it was also also the sole supplier of the iPhone X's display panels and even memory. Back then, the company was making about $110 from each display. This figure ultimately helped Samsung bring in more money from iPhone panels than its own Galaxy S8. So even if its phones may depreciate in value over iPhones, Samsung may have the last laugh, anyway.
Though the two companies have been competitors for quite some time, this hasn't really stopped Apple and Samsung from joining forces. Returning to the example of the iPhone X, it's been estimated that the $110 Samsung made from each display panel has netted the company about $14.3 billion in earnings, based on Apple selling 130 million iPhone X models within 20 months of its release. The company also made $202 from Galaxy S8 components, with reports projecting potential earnings of $10.1 billion based on 50 million devices sold within 20 months of release.
Of course, Apple stopped sharing its sales figures on Macs, iPhones, and iPads back in 2018 – and Samsung isn't a fan of sharing specifics, either. However, it's still rather likely Samsung did pretty well for itself, especially considering that the component side of Samsung's business made up about 35% of the company's revenue in 2016, which was the year prior to the iPhone X's release. Going back to the iPhone Duo, Counterpoint Research expects shipments of folding panels to rise by the end of 2026, with the overall market value growing to $4.4 billion — a 48% increase over 2025.
Though it's a bit too early to tell if the iPhone Duo will make Samsung more money than the Galaxy S26, the company is still expecting to have an extremely profitable second half of the year. It's even made efforts to get a new display production facility up and running. Granted, you might have to wait until 2027 to get an iPhone Duo, but this partnership between rivals could still net Samsung plenty of profit in the long run."

11,2026-10-24,"We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.
Walmart's in-house brand — Onn — known for a wide range of affordable gadgets and accessories has a couple of dash cams in its portfolio. These dash cams, like the brand's other offerings, are relatively inexpensive. While you can get the Onn Full HD 1080p Dash Cam for just $30, the 1080p Dash Cam Front and Rear Set costs only $50. Moreover, in this era of flash storage prices hitting sky-high, Onn actually bundles a microSD card with both models for no extra cost, essentially giving you a gadget that works out of the box.
To give you some context for this pricing, the 70mai M310, which is one of the dash cams well-liked by Amazon shoppers, costs $46 at list price for just its front unit and without any included microSD card. That said, it has a slightly higher 1296p resolution. This affordable pricing certainly makes both Onn dash cam models pretty enticing, but what do these dash cams bring to the table, and are they actually good?
As mentioned, Onn offers two dash camera models. Both are largely similar, except for a couple of notable differences. For example, both dash cams have a 1,920 x 1,080p resolution, as indicated by their names, and feature a 2-inch display for viewing the camera feed and interacting with it. The two also feature a G Sensor to detect any collision incidents to save video recordings, support a microSD card with a capacity of up to 32 GB, and use a suction cup for mounting on the car windshield. 
However, the Onn 1080p Dash Cam has a 130-degree field of view, whereas the 1080p Dash Cam Front and Rear Set has a 140-degree field of view. This affects how much of the road and surrounding area the camera captures. Another difference between the two models is that the 1080p Dash Cam Front and Rear Set includes an additional rear camera to capture what's happening behind your car. Unlike the front camera, the rear camera attaches using an adhesive pad that's also fixed to the camera.
Onn's dash cameras have received mixed reviews from Walmart shoppers. Buyers have rated the 1080p Dash Cam at 3.8 out of 5 and the 1080p Dash Cam Front and Rear Set at 4.2 out of 5. The incentivized buyer reviews of the 1080p Dash Cam are largely positive, and shoppers say the dash cam is easy to use, offers good video quality, and has a compact design. However, the folks who received no incentive to review it don't have such flattering opinions. One buyer says the camera overheats and stops working in hotter climates, such as in Arizona, whereas another buyer complained about the bundled microSD card, which didn't work for them. The dash cam itself stopped working for another shopper. The sole positive feedback from a non-incentivized buyer was about the camera's affordability.
The 1080p Dash Cam Front and Rear Set has only received incentivized reviews, which are mostly positive, with buyers claiming the picture quality is clear and the mounting mechanism works well. That said, a couple of buyers note that the dash cam didn't work for them at all, or they had issues with sticking the rear camera.
For those who are unaware, incentivized reviews are essentially feedback from buyers who were either paid for writing the review or received the product for free, making them not the most unbiased sources of information. Unfortunately, Onn dash cams aren't widely reviewed, with barely any feedback from experts or actual buyers. If you're looking for well-rated options from unbiased experts, there are four dash cams recommended by Consumer Reports that you can consider."
12,2026-10-24,"Bitcoin BTC$84,459.46 treasury companies like Strategy (MSTR) and Strive (ASST), whose purchases help maintain demand for the largest cryptocurrency, have a tough balancing act, according to Fidelity Digital Assets and analysts who spoke to CoinDesk.
On the one hand, they need cash to fund their purchases. On the other, they need to maintain investor appetite for new sales of the perpetual preferred stock that is their favored fundraising vehicle. As the number of shares in circulation grows, so do the dividend payments.
The securities, which sit between debt and common equity in the capital structure with regular payouts and no voting rights, have financed purchases of some 136,000 bitcoin in the 20 months since their introduction, Fidelity research analyst Zack Wainwright wrote in a Tuesday report. That’s more than 0.5% of the cryptocurrency’s maximum supply.
Strategy, the largest corporate bitcoin holder, is the dominant company in the market. Its preferred shares — it has five different classes — make it a bellwether for the model’s durability. Its first offering, STRK, debuted in February 2025. 
Fidelity puts the bitcoin-focused preferred market at roughly $12 billion. The global fixed-income market is $150 trillion, suggesting there’s room for growth.
By selling preferred shares that offer dividends without a fixed maturity, the companies reduce refinancing pressure while attracting investors reluctant to own bitcoin directly.
“Perpetual preferreds have brought bitcoin to income investors, a group that ETFs never reached,” said Mark Palmer, a senior equity research analyst at StoneX.
Ryan Rasmussen, the head of research at Bitwise, also sees the products attracting buyers who might avoid bitcoin exchange-traded funds, though he expects the ETFs to remain the bigger demand channel.
“Over the long run, I expect ETFs to be a larger source of demand for bitcoin, with perpetual preferred offerings acting as a second tailwind addressing a different type of investor (yield investors),” Rasmussen said.
James Butterfill, CoinShares’ head of research, said STRC alone, which pays a variable dividend currently around 12%, raised $7.5 billion during 2026 through late July. That funding carries a recurring dividend cost.
Alex Blume, the founder and CEO of institutional bitcoin asset manager Two Prime, pointed out that products such as STRC and Strive’s SATA don’t offer just exposure to bitcoin prices. Their price also reacts to management competence, issuers’ obligations and capital-market conditions.
He cited STRC briefly falling into the $70 range in late June as evidence of potential volatility. Growing issuance creates a tension between increasing bitcoin per common share, meeting dollar-denominated dividend-payout commitments and avoiding bitcoin sales, he said. The Strategy preferred stock currently trades around $99.40.
“The catch is that the demand for perpetual preferreds is reflexive,” Palmer said. Continued preferred-funded buying depends on the securities trading at or near par, he added. Below par, fresh issuance becomes less attractive, and companies may favor repurchases, Butterfill said.
Strategy’s latest weekly update reported $193 million spent repurchasing STRC alongside purchases of just 267 bitcoin, according to Butterfill. The firm also increased its preferred-stock repurchase authorization to $3 billion, he said.
“Buybacks can reduce future dividend costs, but they compete with bitcoin purchases for capital today,” Butterfill said.
Cash reserves provide a buffer, although Strategy has already sold some bitcoin to fund preferred dividends, he added.
U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs have attracted approximately $57 billion in cumulative net inflows and hold around $106 billion in assets, Butterfill said.
Net ETF creations increase funds’ bitcoin holdings, while issuance of preferred stock gives management discretion over spending. Redemptions can reverse ETF demand but leave no continuing dividend commitment, he said.
Butterfill sees preferreds amplifying the cycle: supporting purchases when financing conditions are favorable, with that support weakening as sentiment deteriorates.

13,2026-10-24,"Yet another busy week for Apple saw a teaser for even more product announcements coming next week, plus we got the highly anticipated launch of Apple's first foldable smartphone, the iPhone Duo.
Other news this week included Verizon announcing a hefty monthly charge for the iPhone Handoff feature that lets two iPhones share the same phone number, while the public release of iOS 27.2 and related updates appears to be right around the corner, so read on below for all of the details on these stories and more!

The Apple launch parade continues, with Apple teasing another set of product introductions next week under the tagline ""Pro and tell."" Apple is holding a media ""experience"" for the new products in Los Angeles on Wednesday, October 28, so we're expecting a wave of press releases that day.
The event tagline and animated logo are suggestive of the long-rumored touchscreen MacBook that looks like it will be appearing as part of the MacBook Pro family rather than as a separate ""MacBook Ultra."" We're also expecting an iPad mini 8 with an OLED display, as well as updated iMac and low-end 14-inch MacBook Pro models featuring the M6 chip. Be sure to check out our overview of launch expectations for more details.

After Apple saw ""extraordinary"" demand for the iPhone Duo on pre-order launch day last week that quickly saw shipping estimates slip all the way until January 2027, some customers held out hope of being able to score a unit on a walk-in basis at an Apple Store when sales began on Friday.
The situation marked a return to long lines at many Apple retail stores, a phenomenon that had largely disappeared in recent years as Apple emphasized offering online orders with pickup appointments rather than first come, first served walk-in sales.
Some retail store staff were advised to discourage walk-in customers from coming in on Friday with expectations of buying the device, and Apple said it would have ""limited"" availability of iPhone Duo in its stores in most countries.
A few lucky customers were able to score walk-in purchases of the iPhone Duo, and Apple appears to be reviving online ordering for in-store pickup that was quickly removed under the crush of pre-order day.

We had been a bit light on details about just what apps would look like on the iPhone Duo's new form factor, but App Store screenshots revealed ahead of the launch how some more of Apple's stock apps have been adapted for the new device.
Early media reviews also gave us a bit better look at the situation, with many noting that third-party apps may not look great until developers get them updated, as by default they continue to show in a traditional iPhone aspect ratio and layout surrounded by black bars.

With iOS 27, Apple introduced a new feature called iPhone Handoff that lets you quickly swap back-and-forth between two phones with one phone number. The feature seems particularly convenient for the iPhone Duo launch, with some users perhaps wanting to carry different iPhone form factors at different times depending on their needs.
iPhone Handoff does require carrier support, which is still quite limited, and carriers can charge extra for those looking to take advantage of the feature. T-Mobile was the first U.S. carrier to add support for it with pricing set at $5 per month, and Verizon has now announced that it too will be adding iPhone Handoff support but at a heftier $10-per-month price point.

Apple this week seeded the release candidates of iOS 27.2 and related updates for testing purposes, suggesting a full public release could come next week.
The beta period was used to test a revamped Health app, but that has been removed for the public release as Apple will continue testing it before rolling it out to everyone later this year. Other feature additions include a new icon and theme for Apple Music alongside a just-announced rebrand, user profiles in the Apple TV app, and an expansion of Siri AI support to new languages.

Apple's new HomeView smart home hub hasn't made it into customers' hands yet, but we're hearing continued rumors about a more advanced version that could arrive in the next year or two.
According to Bloomberg's Mark Gurman, this ""HomeView Max"" will include a 9-inch display (up from 7 inches in the standard HomeView) and include a robotic arm that will allow the display to swivel a full 360º to maintain visibility of the screen and keep subjects in view during video calls as they move around a room.

15,2026-10-24,"This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Siobhan MacDermott, a 56-year-old tech founder based in Menlo Park, California. It's been edited for length and clarity.
I've never been underqualified for any job that I've applied to.
I was vice chair at Bank of America and principal at EY. I held senior roles in cybersecurity at AVG and McAfee. I've sat in boardrooms with Fortune 500 CEOs, advised governments, and built practices from scratch.
Then, in early 2024, when I was 54, I had to relocate from New York City to California for family reasons, leaving the management consulting job I'd held as a partner at The Consello Group. I didn't anticipate what could happen when someone tries to re-enter the institutional world in their 50s — particularly as a woman in a career space that doesn't fit neatly into a job description.
The most surprising barrier has been the gap between what I've actually done and what the market knows how to categorize. I'm worried because I'm not sure where the economy is heading, but I'm an absolute eternal optimist.
I'm not going to accept anything other than staying hopeful.
In Still in the Game, Business Insider is chronicling the realities of job hunting in your 50s — from overcoming age bias and navigating career transitions to rebuilding confidence, updating skills, and finding new opportunities in a competitive job market. Read more stories here, and share your own experience here.
It's been frustrating to watch organizations that desperately need exactly what I've built not hire me.
When I started my job search, I reached out to my network to try to figure out what I could do, who'd vouch for me, and where open positions were. A lot of senior roles were being downsized, but I had a lot of people who initially wanted to help me find something.
The strategies that were a complete waste of time were LinkedIn Easy Apply, recruiter calls that went nowhere, and any process that involved a 45-minute competency-based interview with someone who'd never heard of the big companies I've worked for.
I spent the better portion of every day for over a year applying and looking for jobs. I obsessively searched on LinkedIn for new positions, applying over and over again on the site, company websites, emails, and more, but getting nowhere.
I sent out countless résumés and barely got any responses. These are jobs that I know I'm qualified or overqualified to do, but sometimes I didn't even get the courtesy of the automated ""thank you for applying"" response.
It was like my application went into the ether. I'd try to find the head of HR for these companies on LinkedIn and reach out to them because I wanted the feedback.
The strategy that worked the best during my job search was bypassing HR entirely and using direct outreach to CEOs and board members.
I'd send them letters not even related to a job, but more of an observation of their company to get their attention. I tried to be crafty, saying, ""Based on my external assessment of your company, here are some things that I think you should be doing as you think through your next quarter.""
Reaching out to CEOs got me in the door, but then they'd send me down to somebody who was pretty junior who'd say, ""You're overqualified for this job."" They'd courtesy interview me because they'd been told to, but it didn't go beyond that.
Create a BI Job Scout and get opportunities matched to your goals.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
Sometimes a CEO would send me to HR, and HR would look at my skill set and say, ""It doesn't quite match.""
That happened quite a few times.
I changed my gender to male and back to female a bunch of times to see if it made a difference — I didn't change my pronouns, just my actual gender. I felt like I had more responses on LinkedIn being thought to be male than female.
I also think being in Silicon Valley, you age out a lot faster than you do in other parts of the US, and it's definitely a highly male- dominated space. I had those two things working against me from the get-go.
So I removed the first six years of my career from my work experience on LinkedIn, and I took the dates off of my degrees so that people couldn't tell my age. This made me seem seven to 10 years younger, potentially. Once I took those dates off, I felt like I got more engagement on the site.
I started my company out of necessity, and also out of intellectual boredom. I felt like my brain was starting to rot.
It happened about a year into job searching, when I started feeling financial pressure and frustration. I didn't want my full-time job to be looking for a job. My search wasn't looking like it was going anywhere in the foreseeable future, especially out in Silicon Valley. If you're a computer scientist or an engineer, there are jobs available, but I couldn't find a lot out here not in that space.
I started thinking, what am I good at? What do I know? What are all the pieces that I could put together? What problem can I identify that needs to be solved? That came together pretty quickly, and I launched two companies — Khaos Holdings, a strategic risk-intelligence company, and TeenAegis, a child-safety intelligence business, in January.
We're raising capital, and if the capital comes in, that will be the end of my job search. If the capital doesn't come in, then I'll keep searching — but I've decided that whatever I do will be adjacent to the problem I'm trying to solve now.
Being out of work for two years shifted my retirement timeline eight to 10 years from digging into savings. However, since my job search started, I've really homed in on what I want to do with the rest of my career.
The journey I went on was a little bit of a midlife crisis. I asked myself, ""What's my purpose in life?"" as opposed to ""What job do I want to have?"" and then identified what skill set could bring me to that.
What led me to where I am was really thinking through questions like: What are all the issues that matter to me? What legacy do I want to leave? How do I want to think about my future for the next 10 or 15 years? How do I want to show up every day?
In the beginning, I was going to apply to anything that I could qualify for that would get me some money. Now I'm not going to do that because I don't think that's going to actually be fulfilling or make me happy. My sense of stability has required a deliberate reframe — from ""security comes from a salary"" to ""security comes from what I've built and what I know.""
The hardest thing is maintaining a positive attitude day after day. If my company doesn't work out, then it's not like I can stop trying.
Taking mental health breaks is important because it's overwhelming, lonely, and hard. You lose your sense of self-worth when you're in this position; people look at you like, ""Oh, you're the person who's still looking for a job."" So it's hard to continue to put yourself out there.
Still, I do believe there's light at the end of the tunnel. It's just the question of how long the tunnel is.
Do you have a story to share about navigating your career in your 50s? If so, please reach out to the editor, Manseen Logan, at mlogan@businessinsider.com.

An NYPD officer shot and wounded a woman Friday night after she approached police with a knife during a night of violence at a Queens home, police said.
The woman, who police have not yet identified, is in stable condition, the NYPD said.
Officials say police responded to calls of a home invasion and stabbing on 149th Street at around 9:40 Friday night. When officers arrived, no one answered the door but two men came to a second-floor window. One was injured, police said, and told officers his uncle had stabbed him and was downstairs.
Eventually, a woman on the third floor, who told police she too was afraid to go downstairs, threw keys out a window, NYPD Chief of Patrol Philip Rivera said during a press briefing.
Police said they heard screaming as they entered and were climbing the stairs. They found two men and a woman struggling in a small bedroom on the third floor in a “chaotic scene.” After officers identified themselves, they ordered the three to raise their hands. The two men complied, but the woman began moving toward the officers with the knife, Rivera said.
“The female still did not comply and moved in the direction of the officers with a knife in her hand,” Rivera told reporters. “She was within several feet of the officers when an officer discharged his firearm, striking her. She fell to the ground. An officer deployed his taser, and another officer recovered the knife.”
Rivera said officers carried the woman downstairs and gave her first aid.
The woman had stab wounds, as did three men in the home, according to police. They were all taken to local hospitals but police didn’t specify their conditions.
Rivera said police don’t believe the wounded woman was responsible for the home invasion or the initial stabbing reported in the 911 calls. The people in the home knew each other, he said, but police weren’t sure how they were related.
Investigators recovered two knives at the scene. The shooting was captured on officers’ body-worn cameras and is being investigated by the NYPD’s Force Investigation Division.
The circumstances of the stabbings and what happened inside the home before officers arrived remain under investigation.
This story is based on preliminary information provided by the NYPD and may be updated.

      Brian Mann
    


                Fewer women in their 20s are choosing to become mothers, leading to an overall birthrate decline in the U.S. But the birth rate among U.S. women age 30-45 is rising according to the latest federal data.
                
                    
                    CHARLY TRIBALLEAU/AFP
                    
                
hide caption

Sarah Harris was 36 years old when she gave birth to her daughter Bridget last year. She describes motherhood as equal parts joy and exhaustion. ""When you have a baby in your mid-30s, what you gain in wisdom and experience you also lose in terms of energy,"" Harris says with a laugh.  
Harris, who lives in Vermont, was laid off from a job four months after Bridget was born. She says she's still figuring out what her career and her life will look like with a child. Harris thinks a lot of women are like her.  They're wrestling with financial uncertainty and waiting much later to choose pregnancy.   
There's also the high cost of raising a child, which Harris said convinced her that one is enough. ""I think in another era or another life or another scenario, I might have had more children,"" Harris says. ""I think we're in a difficult societal moment and so that's absolutely affecting people's reproductive choices.""
Harris is, in fact, part of a slow revolution in what motherhood and parenting look like in the United States  The latest report from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention found the general fertility rate for women fell by 1% to a new record low in 2025. That continues a two-decade trend toward fewer children.
The one-year drop for 20-somethings was even more eye-catching. ""Certainly the 6% decline in the birthrate for women age 20 to 24 is noteworthy,"" said Brady Hamilton, a demographer at the CDC's National Center for Health Statistics who co-authored the report. He noted that women in their late teens also saw a 7% decline. 
A separate study released last month by the Federal Reserve Bank of Atlanta found Gen Z women, in their late teens and twenties, ""are dramatically less likely to want children"" compared with U.S. women of previous generations.
""Several factors have been identified as contributing to this lower desire to have children, such as increased labor market opportunities and agency among women, rising financial costs, changing priorities, and [concerns about] maternal mortality,"" the study found.
Dr. Tanise Branche, medical director of labor and delivery at Temple Health, a health, research and training network in Philadelphia, told NPR more of her younger women patients are following a different life script. 
""They're starting to focus not necessarily just on what they can bring to kids, but what's going to be best for them,"" she says.
Meanwhile, the latest CDC data shows a small but significant increase in older women, ages 30 to 45, who are choosing to become pregnant.  
Branche says more women, including herself, are building careers and stable lives before becoming mothers. 
Branche is currently pregnant with her fourth child. ""I will be 43 with my last delivery and I started this reproductive journey at 37,"" she said.  ""I see the net positive being that we have a choice."" 
As a consequence of this shift, there are fewer children now in the U.S.  The population under age 18 is falling steadily, according to the U.S. Census Bureau, dropping by more than 1 million from 2010 to 2020. 
Some experts also believe women would choose to have more kids if they could afford them and had proper supports that include affordable housing, daycare and health insurance.
""The fact that there are a lot of people who still say they want families, but don't feel like they're able to have them [is a concern],"" says Anna Louie Sussman, author of a new book on parenting choices called Inconceivable.  ""Young people say, I can barely take care of myself I can't imagine taking care of someone else.""
Sussman herself is part of the trend toward delayed motherhood. Because of her own concerns about affordability and her career, she froze her eggs and waited to have her child until she was in her early 40s.  
""I do feel lucky that I'm a little further along professionally than I would have been in my 20s or even 30s,"" she said.
As women build different lives and birthrates keep falling, the U.S. is changing.  The median age is rising rapidly, reaching 39.4 years old in 2025.  More states, including Vermont where Sarah Harris lives, are seeing actual population decline, according to the Census Bureau. 
""I think about it a lot because I live in a very small place where we're hoping to keep the doors of our elementary school open for as long as we can,"" she says.
A previous version of this story mistakenly stated Sarah Harris’ age as 37 when she gave birth to her first child. She was 36.

18,2026-10-24,"New York City was home to my husband and me for six years before we had kids and moved to Asheville, and it will forever be a place that holds so much for us. That's why when the timing, cost, and opportunity aligned for our family of four to vacation there for a week recently, I was chomping at the bit to return, enthusiastic at the prospect of sharing this colossal, important place with our 4- and 6-year-old daughters.
It was easy to have high expectations for a trip down memory lane, but the reality is that any trip with kids will be what it will be, and we can only hope that something along the way — a bump in the road, a detour, a singalong — cements into their memories and crystallizes into sentimental gemstones that they turn over and over in their minds with time.
Leading up to departure, I planned a weeklong itinerary with suggestions for each day on where to go, what to eat, who to see, and what to do. Knowing my kids would need some rest and recovery, I built in plenty of downtime — or so I thought. Still, as with all things parenting, I was rudely reminded that even with my experience and expertise as Mom, I could not foresee every breakdown.
By midweek, my daughters hit a physical, mental, and emotional wall. I had planned to take them to the Met, but instead, I was met with loud, convincing pleas to spend a couple of hours in the hotel room. Crestfallen, I agreed. They didn't know about the daydreams I'd had of walking them through the museum to see the Temple of Dendur, the Medieval Sculpture Hall, and rooms where Degas's ballerinas, Monet's water lilies, or Van Gogh's self-portrait lived. Sigh. I'm an art lover — I enjoy meandering through vast halls and admiring human creativity and intellect. It brings me great peace and joy.
A few days later, I found myself in the same situation at the Whitney. My generally agreeable kids protested once again, so we switched gears. We took a stroll on the High Line, grabbed pizza from Chelsea Market, and landed at the Diller — von Furstenberg Sundeck & Water Feature between 14th and 15th Streets. There, they were all smiles — delighting in the sun, in the relief of cool water, barefoot, holding up their dresses, splashing, squealing, happy.
My kids are still young — 4 and 6 — and have only experienced small art museums. They don't even know what they're missing! So, naturally, when it was clear that we weren't going to visit any art museums, reality stung. This is one of the painful aspects of parenting: accepting that the things you really want to do just don't happen for one reason or another. Of course, you could force your child to do what you'd like to, but what memory does that create? What good does that serve?
I let out another deep sigh and told myself, It's OK. There's always next time — hopefully. I smiled, thinking about the next time, and I began to daydream once again. All was well. What mattered most to me on this trip was visiting the place we once called home and introducing it to our girls — acquainting them with the place their mother and father made countless memories, befriended incredible people, grew stronger in their marriage, became parents during a pandemic, and tearfully bid adieu.
Parenting continues to be one wide and winding journey, where I'm constantly toeing the line between curating memories and allowing memories to make themselves in the most ordinary of places: on the 32nd floor of a hotel room, on a playground in Central Park, on a Subway train, on a city bus, on an airplane in the clouds.
In addition, I'm still learning that parenting is mostly a selfless act. Yes, there are moments we get to share with our kids that allow them to see what fills us up as human beings, but it's often an unwieldy and imperfect balance.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
Many of the decisions we make come at a cost, and, for this trip (and future trips), I'd rather the cost be one that I pay — not them. Their joy is my joy. And, in this chapter of life, it's bigger, grander, more powerful than any selfish indulgence that life has ever afforded me, and, honestly, I'm here for it. I'm grateful.

Flashforge Creator 5 is a four head tool changer built for fast multicolor and multi material printing, with purgeless tool changes and a heated chamber for engineering filaments like ABS, PA, and ASA. The four independent heads also add redundancy, so a head issue does not stop every print. The main tradeoffs are price, a slower current firmware experience, and the need for a prime tower, which still leaves some waste. It also shakes enough that lower acceleration may help on taller prints, making it a strong but specialized upgrade.
Multicolor print shops needing faster tool changes, because four independent heads cut multicolor print time and avoid purge waste.
Users printing engineering filaments in an enclosed machine, because the heated chamber suits PA and ASA while the tool changer handles multi-material jobs.
Makers who value low-waste color changes, because the printer can run purgeless operations with multiple filament types in one print.
Builders who want redundancy in a production setup, because one head can be repaired while the other three keep printing.
Shoppers expecting low-waste multicolor printing to be cheap, because purge savings are small compared with the printer's price premium.
Single-nozzle buyers expecting simple filament swaps, because this machine needs manual feeding and cutter use while nozzle access takes multiple screws.
Tall-print hobbyists running aggressive acceleration, because the frame shakes enough to cause taller prints to fail.
People who want a plug-and-play multicolor machine, because current firmware is slow and still requires a prime tower for priming.
The printer can ship from US locations and may arrive in about three days for some purchases.
The printer can produce multicolor and ABS parts after setup, with print quality described as amazing.
The printer's four separate heads enable purgeless operation and support multiple filament types within one print.

Yes, the deal listings discussed here ship from a US-based warehouse, and some orders reportedly came from a US Amazon warehouse. That usually means faster delivery and fewer surprise fees than China-shipped listings.
It uses four independent tool heads, so multicolor printing does not rely on filament swapping and can avoid the usual ""poop"" waste. A prime tower is still used for priming, so some waste remains.
The Creator 5 is faster for multicolor because tool changes take about 5 seconds, while the AD5X needs filament swaps that can take over a minute. The AD5X has automatic filament feeding, cutting, and retracting, but the Creator 5 is better for low-waste tool-changing prints.
It can be worth it if print time matters, because multicolor jobs can finish about a day sooner than on an AD5X. The tradeoff is a much higher upfront price, and the filament savings alone are too small to offset that cost quickly.

JetBlue seems to be inching ever-closer to a Chapter 11 bankruptcy protection filing. The airline hasn’t turned a profit since before the pandemic, and it seems like such a filing is inevitable, as debt will need to be restructured. The company’s CEO has promised it won’t happen in 2026, though I’d be shocked if it doesn’t happen in 2027. Along those lines, there’s a noteworthy update, as clearly there’s a recent vibe shift among creditors…
In this post:
The Wall Street Journal reports how a group of senior creditors to JetBlue is working with a financial adviser and law firm, as the airline grapples with cash burn and increased jet fuel prices. JetBlue’s stock is trading at new lows in recent days, and closed Friday at $2.62, well under 50% of where it was trading under three months ago.
Not only that, but bonds are trading down as well. The company’s $2 billion notes due in 2031 fell to 42 cents on the dollar earlier this week, compared to trading near 80 cents on the dollar earlier this month.
In a statement, JetBlue simply shared that the company “remains confident in its JetForward ​strategy and the progress being ​made ⁠toward returning to sustained profitability.” Of course that says nothing about the possibility of filing for Chapter 11 bankruptcy. Quite to the contrary, a Chapter 11 filing is almost certainly a key part of what will allow the airline to have a chance of success in the long run.
As mentioned above, JetBlue hasn’t turned a profit since before the start of the pandemic. The airline has also had a series of setbacks, ranging from its partnership with American being blocked by regulators, and then its merger with Spirit also being blocked by regulators. All the while, executives at the airline did nothing to actually improve the carrier’s independent prospects.
Fortunately that’s starting to change, as leadership increasingly puts effort into its TrueBlue program, introduces first class while reconfiguring planes more efficiently, and opens lounge.
I trust that JetBlue will eventually be fine. The issue is, the airline now has close to $9 billion in debt. So even if the airline could at some point turn an operating profit, servicing that amount of debt while being a relatively small carrier seems like a hurdle that’s not easy to overcome.
Just to put that into context, Delta only has about double as much debt as JetBlue, despite having around seven times as much revenue. Meanwhile Southwest has materially less debt than JetBlue, despite having more than three times as much revenue.
For years we’ve known that United is at least in theory interested in JetBlue in some form… mainly its presence at New York (JFK). While for a while it sure seemed like United wanted a merger with JetBlue, clearly management has decided against that with current debt levels. If JetBlue does enter bankruptcy protection, though, I wouldn’t be surprised if United is part of some long term deal.
A group of JetBlue creditors have hired advisers, as the carrier’s stock and bonds sink. JetBlue hasn’t been in a good spot in a long time, as the company hasn’t turned a profit since before the pandemic. But we’re getting to the point where JetBlue can’t keep reporting operating losses while also having such a big debt burden.
JetBlue’s CEO had promised there wouldn’t be a bankruptcy filing in 2026, but it seems like the goal has almost been to just wait and see if any airline tries for a merger. With the current debt levels, it seems that even United isn’t interested, but I imagine that could change once the carrier is in Chapter 11.
How do you see this JetBlue situation playing out?

Apple had resisted releasing a touchscreen laptop for years – despite the technology seeing plenty of success in the PC market 

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The late Apple co-founder Steve Jobs had a reputation for being ahead of the curve. At the time, Microsoft was heavily pushing touchscreen computing in an attempt to innovate and embrace new possibilities when interfacing with machines; Jobs dug his heels in and refused to take Apple down that same path.
Steve Jobs was speaking during the 'Back to the Mac' media event at Apple's corporate headquarters while introducing Mac OS X 10.7 Lion.
This article is part of TechRadar Pro's QOTD project to provide an insight into the minds of the brightest and most recognized figures in the technology industry today and in years gone by. Read the full series here.
Instead of adding a touchscreen to the MacBook, which Jobs saw as something of a gimmick, Apple focused on multi-touch gestures using the trackpad and mice instead of adding a touchscreen into the fabric of the display. The reason, according to Jobs, was simple: using this technology would quickly lead to strain, fatigue, and the loss of precision the longer a user was navigating using touch.
He put his foot down despite immense industry pressure at the time in the form of the emergence of new kinds of devices such as the HP Touchsmart family.
With Windows continuing to introduce new touchscreen devices and 2-in-1s to the market, Apple stood firm on its resolve over the next few years – but the manufacturer experimented with bridge technologies like the touchbar, which was eventually phased out.
There has since been renewed pressure on Apple to embrace touchscreen and embed this in its devices – with the company now expected to break its stance for the first time and introduce a new touchscreen MacBook Pro with an OLED panel, according to rumors.
