He has worked as a technology journalist for more than five years, having previously held the role of features editor with ITPro. In his previous role, he oversaw the commissioning and publishing of long form in areas including AI, cyber security, cloud computing and digital transformation.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

These days, it's exceedingly difficult not to be jaded and weary about the tech market. Memory, graphics cards, and now storage are becoming impossible to obtain; there's basically no new consumer hardware being released; and there's AI everywhere. Technology YouTuber TrashBench (TB), too, is tired and was harkening back to the halcyon days when everything was fresh and straightforward — so he went back in time with a retro build.
More specifically, he recreated a facsimile of the first machine his parents bought him in 1996 when he was a wee lad, to play The Need for Speed: Special Edition — a PC-improved version of the first ever game in the series. According to the man, it was thanks to this machine that he fell in love with computers in general.
TB kicked things off with an Intel Pentium MMX 200 MHz CPU sitting atop an Epox Pronix EP-P55-BT motherboard, boasting what was at the time a new, fresh, convenient ZIF (Zero Insertion Force) socket. This motherboard popped up during the RAM transition from SIMM to DIMM, which is why you'll see two slots of each on the top. 32 MB of RAM went right in, a generous amount for the year.
Funnily enough, the CPU barely needed cooling, which is why a simple 40-mm fan suffices, although anyone who's lived through this era will tell you that those were guaranteed to start whining after just a few months of heavy usage. There is no case cooling, as it's just not needed.
However, he did need to correctly set the motherboard's jumpers, as such luxuries like automatically detecting CPU speed and voltage were just becoming normal. Yes, this meant that you could burn out a CPU if you played the jumper game wrong.
For graphics card duty, he enlisted an S3 Trio32 card, which was pretty, ahem, modest even in the era, and had no 3D rendering capabilities whatsoever. In this era, the now-ubiquitous 3D accelerator cards were just coming into fashion, and he eventually replaced the S3 with a Matrox Mystique 3D, another offering of dubious quality. Yet those were happy days, as any form of 3D acceleration was far better than none.
Storage comes by way of a Western Digital Caviar 22100 hard drive with a whopping two gigabytes of storage and the uncanny ability to emulate the dulcet tones of a jackhammer. TB eventually swapped it later for a roomier 80 GB unit. Again, due to the idiosyncrasies and constant transitions of the era — hardware doubled in speed and capacity nearly every year — he had to set a jumper to clip it to the likely amounts of either 8.4 GB or 33.4 GB, as the addressing space of BIOSes and disk controllers of the time were limited.
Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.
The build wraps up with an OG Sound Blaster 16 sound card and a CD-ROM drive. After test-driving the machine with Need for Speed, he moved on to Microsoft Flight Simulator 98, which necessitated a joystick. Naturally, he got the much-beloved Quickshot QS-123, a staple in many home PCs, and one that needed replacement in about six months, as its build quality matched its price.

A career change for the GeForce RTX 5090

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

If you are wondering why the GeForce RTX 5090, despite being one of the best graphics cards, has evaporated from retail shelves, the answer lies in China’s industrial conversion factories. A newly surfaced video reveals the staggering scale of the country’s grey-market AI operations, showing industrial workshops dismantling brand-new GeForce RTX 5090 graphics cards to repurpose them for the AI boom.
 




So, what exactly is a GeForce RTX 5090 doing in China when the graphics card is not even officially available there? The answer is simple. They are there to be transformed into AI workhorses. The recently leaked footage offers a glimpse inside these Chinese workshops, where skilled technicians meticulously desolder the pricey flagship’s GB202 silicon and GDDR7 memory chips. They then transplant the components into a dual-slot blower design tailored for server use.
The GeForce RTX 5090 blower graphics cards will ultimately end up in high-density server racks where they will become the backbone of massive AI compute clusters. The scale and efficiency of these operations are impressive, with production lines converting shipments of brand-new GeForce RTX 5090 graphics cards, from Nvidia's custom partner models to the chipmaker's highly coveted Founders Edition. After testing and validation, workers would repackage these modified GeForce RTX 5090 graphics cards and ship them to clients, likely data centers or AI labs.
Many of these Chinese factories are scattered throughout the country. The conversion process is massive in scale, and probably thousands of GeForce RTX 5090 graphics cards are being repurposed every day. Middlemen in these grey-market supply chains are constantly hunting for GeForce RTX 5090 stock to fuel these operations.
【太精彩了吧 😱】近日，社交媒體 X 上流出多段顯示卡改裝工場內部影片，可以看到大量 GeForce RTX 5090 頂級遊戲顯示卡並未流向一般消費級零售市場，而是被整批運往大型改裝工廠。廠內工人在具備完整流水線的作業環境下，進行大規模拆解與改裝，將原本針對電競設計的三風扇散熱架構全數替換為 Dual Slot 渦輪散熱器，以符合 AI 伺服器機箱的高密度排列需求。
https://t.co/2wzPmweU3s
從曝光的工廠現場畫面可見，流水線工作枱上堆滿了來自各大廠商的 GeForce RTX 5090 零售彩盒包裝與全新裸卡，甚至連 NVIDIA 自家 GeForce RTX 5090 FE 也有。工人正以熟練的工序，迅速卸除原廠龐大的風扇與散熱鰭片模組，將完整的 PCB 電路板分離出來。隨後，GPU 與 VRAM 會被「吹」出來移植至另一片 PCB 上，再統一換裝上厚度僅有 2-Slot 的厚銅底座渦輪鼓風散熱系統。
改裝完畢後，廠方隨即將顯示卡送往專門的測試檢驗產線。在多組相連的開放式測試平台上，數十張已換裝渦輪散熱器的顯示卡正同步運行圖形效能負載與穩定性跑分測試，確保每張改裝後的繪圖卡皆能正常運作。通過嚴格檢測的顯示卡將被分層整齊陳列並重新裝箱，準備直接交付給相關客戶，流程非常專業。
傳統消費級高階顯示卡通常採用三風扇大型散熱設計，厚度普遍佔用 3 至 4 個插槽，且出風方向為機箱內部側吹，難以在 4U 伺服器或多卡並聯環境中並排安裝。換裝雙槽渦輪散熱系統後，散熱器能利用鼓風風扇將廢熱直接排出機箱後方，大幅提升多卡伺服器內部的散熱效率與空間相容性。
面對 AI 運算需求持續殷切，在算力緊缺的情況下，GeForce RTX 5090 遊戲顯示卡依然是 AI 算力市場的熱門替代品。目前，GeForce RTX 5090 售價已經被炒上接近一萬美元水平，這個價位遊戲玩家根本無力負擔。October 23, 2026
Nvidia has reportedly halted production of the GeForce RTX 5090 and is, in all likelihood, redirecting the valuable silicon to higher-margin professional GPUs like the RTX Pro 6000 Blackwell. At the same time, a vast network of Chinese conversion factories continues to operate at full speed. Redirected supply and aggressive grey-market recycling have nearly wiped out GeForce RTX 5090 stock from U.S. retailers.
Custom GeForce RTX 5090 graphics cards now start at an eye-watering $8,500. However, a new workaround has emerged to sidestep the stock shortage and inflated prices: prebuilt gaming systems. Sometimes these GeForce RTX 5090 prebuilts cost less than a standalone GeForce RTX 5090, while offering a fully assembled system with a manufacturer's warranty.
If you had told me a few months ago that buying an entire gaming system would be cheaper than a single high-end graphics card, I would have called you insane. Yet here we are. In today’s chaotic market, prebuilt systems often offer better value than standalone graphics cards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

You really shouldn’t have to overlook the low-rent control options or the unwelcome hiss that’s audible when the system should be silent, but if and when you do, you’ll quite quickly realize that the Fyne Audio Cubitt 5 is among the very best pound-for-pound options around where sub-$750 wireless stereo speaker systems are concerned.
Spacious, organized and poised sound
Plenty of inputs
Good standard of build and finish
Boticeably hissy when powered on
No on-system controls, no input or volume indictors
Cheap-looking and -feeling remote control

Why you can trust TechRadar




We spend hours testing every product or service we review, so you can be sure you're buying the best. Find out more about how we test.

It’s taken Fyne Audio less than a decade to establish itself as a deeply credible manufacturer of passive loudspeakers at a wide range of prices. The Cubitt 5, though, represents its first pair of active speakers — and they've got some serious competition to overcome.
At first glance, though, the system seems to have every chance. Its compact, well-made cabinets are available in a wide range of finishes, and are home to an intriguing driver array that draws liberally from the company’s very successful (and much more expensive) line-up of passive loudspeakers. Power is more than adequate at 240 watts, and the range of inputs (Bluetooth with aptX HD capability, line- and MM phono-level analog, optical and HDMI ARC) is very competitive, too. The lack of on-system controls is an odd decision, as is the lack confirmation of selected input or volume, and the remote handset that’s your only control option is unlovely.
Get the system up and running and you’ll have to ignore the unwelcome low-volume hiss that comes from each speaker before any music is cued up — fortunately it’s not an issue once your tracks are playing. Instead you’re free to admire the Cubitt 5’s abilities with detail retrieval, soundstaging, low-frequency authority and articulate midrange communication. It’s absolutely as confident as is acceptable when it comes to handling the top of the frequency range, but even here it’s not overtly ill-balanced or unruly.
Add in significant dynamic headroom, an expansive overall presentation and a disinclination to alter its stance in any significant way no matter which of its input options is in use, and it’s apparent that where the fundamentals are concerned, the Cubitt 5 has plenty going for it. Maybe not quite for the best stereo speakers on the market, though.
 



The Fyne Audio Cubitt 5 wireless stereo speaker system is on sale now, and in the United States it costs $749. In the United Kingdom it sells for £549, while in Australia you’ll have to part with AU$1,499.
The idea of positioning a tweeter in the acoustic center of a mid or mid/bass driver is not a new one, but it’s a rarity nevertheless, and Fyne Audio has been busily democratizing its ‘IsoFlare’ variation on this particular theme for a while now. The Cubitt 5 is just the latest example.
In this instance there’s a 19mm titanium dome tweeter positioned in the center of a 127mm multi-fiber mid/bass driver — the idea, as ever, is to create a single point-source of sound at the same time as optimizing driver integration and sound dispersion. The mid/bass driver’s surround is the company’s ‘FyneFlute’ design, the unusual pattern of which helps minimize unwanted energy from the pistonic movement of the driver. Each of the four drivers gets 60 watts of Class D amplification to power it — an all-in total of 240 watts really should be adequate in a system such as this — and the output is bolstered by a front-firing bass reflex slot. It’s an arrangement Fyne Audio suggests will deliver a frequency response of 43Hz - 34kHz.
Sign up for breaking news, reviews, opinion, top tech deals, and more.
The primary speaker takes care of all wired and wireless connectivity. The wireless stuff consists of Bluetooth 5.0, with codec compatibility up to and including aptX HD. Physical connections, all on the rear panel, run to a moving magnet (or high-output moving coil) phono stage accessed via stereo RCA inputs, a line-level analog equivalent also using RCAs, a digital optical input and an HDMI ARC socket. All incoming digital information is dealt with by a DAC of 24bit/96kHz resolution.
 



There’s a unique aspect to the way the Fyne Audio Cubitt 5 performs — or, at least, it’s unique in my extensive experience of products of this type. Connect the speakers together using the supplied umbilical, plug the primary speaker into the mains, turn the power on, and there’s an obvious and consistent hiss from both speakers. Is it loud? No, but it can be heard down the length of my listening room. Is it audible when playing music? No, not at all. So is it an issue? I guess not, unless you intend to use the speakers on your desktop, but I can’t pretend it fills me with confidence.
Which is a great pity, because when it’s actually playing music the Cubitt 5 is one of the best sub-$750 wireless stereo speaker systems I’ve heard so far. In virtually every respect, it’s an enjoyable, balanced and well-sorted listen.
With a vinyl copy of Julian Cope’s Peggy Suicide incoming via the moving magnet phono stage, the Fyne Audio doesn’t take its eye off the ball for a moment. It’s good where detail retrieval is concerned, and manages to stay on top of the finest details without sacrificing any sense of unity or performance. It creates a fairly large soundstage — it’s certainly larger than the cabinets that are producing it — and it’s coherent, organized and easy to understand.
The tonal balance is persuasive, too, the Cubitt 5 hits the plosives at the top of the frequency range absolutely as hard as it dares, but somehow they never quite get too edgy or overly hard. The midrange is sweetly communicative, so there’s confidence and eloquence to the way the system handles a vocalist. And while the claims for extension down to 43Hz are, I think it’s fair to say, a false alarm, the Cubitt 5 nevertheless bigs deep, hits hard and controls its low-frequency activity well. Rhythmic expression is very decent as a result, and the way the system handles tempos is similarly unforced and naturalistic.
There’s a fair amount of dynamic headroom available, so the Fyne Audio handles big shifts in volume or intensity calmly, and its overall attention to detail means that the more subtle dynamics of harmonic variation are given their due weighting, too. Frequency response is nice and even, too. The tendency to attack the highest frequencies can make it sound like the top end is being overstated on occasion, but otherwise the Cubitt 5 gives every area of the frequency range fair representation.
It’s winningly unconcerned about volume, too. Some systems can change their character when asked to play at bigger levels, but the Fyne Audio just gets louder. There’s no change in tonal balance or frequency response, and the stage maintains its shape, too. And the same is true when you change inputs — switch to a wireless stream of John Glacier’s Money Shows and the fundamental attitude and character of the system don’t alter in the slightest. It’s still balanced, insightful, punchy and just slightly treble-happy.
 



It’s by no means an easy trick to pull off, but the Cubitt 5 manages to look discreet and interesting at the same time.
The ‘discreet’ part is mostly thanks to the 260 x 165 x 215mm (HxWxD) dimensions, assisted by some nicely curved cabinet corners and a selection of no fewer than five very agreeable finishes. My sample is in ‘olive green’, but ‘arctic white’, ‘midnight black’, ‘pearl titanium’ and ‘pebble gray’ are also available. No matter which finish you decide suits you best, you’ll find it’s the final flourish in a process that includes a very high standard of construction. There are no visible seams, and the soft-touch paint is flawlessly applied.
‘Interesting’ is mostly derived from the driver array that’s already been discussed in the ‘features’ section of this review, as well as the forward-facing bass reflex slot that somehow makes each speaker’s appearance easy to anthropomorphize. The not-quite-circular brightwork surrounding the driver array doesn’t do any harm when it comes to making this most functional of products look interesting, either. It comes as no surprise to me that Fyne Audio doesn’t see the need to supply the Cubitt 5 with grilles.
 



In terms of getting up and running, there’s really not a lot to get your head around here. Despite the fact this is an active system, only the primary speaker requires mains power — it transmits power, as well as data, along a supplied four-core umbilical cable that screws down onto the rear of each speaker. Once the speakers are connected together and the primary is powered up, it’s really only a question of positioning them to your satisfaction and then you’re in business.
Getting what you want from the system isn’t all that tricky, either, but neither is it all that satisfying an experience. This is not a Wi-Fi-enabled system, of course, so there’s no control app, which is hardly unheard-of in this part of the market. There are no controls on either speaker, either, which is rather less common. The primary speaker has an LED tell-tale that’s red when the system’s in standby and blue when it’s powered up. The blue light flashes when it’s following instructions from the remote handset that’s your only method of operation.
Given that the remote is all you’ve got, though, it’s a shame Fyne Audio specified what I imagine is the least expensive handset it could source. The look and feel is down-market, and the fact you’ve no indication of the source that’s been selected or the volume level that’s been specified only adds to the impression of cost- and corner-cutting.
 



Judged purely on the sound it makes, there’s no denying the Cubitt 5 offers very decent value for money indeed — once it’s making a sound, that is. The standard of build and finish only adds to the impression of money well spent.
But there’s more to it than this, of course — and as far as the experiential goes, the feel of operating the system, the sensation is undeniably low-rent. Which unfortunately undermines the impression of value the Fyne Audio system otherwise offers.

4 / 5
You want full-range sound from winningly compact cabinetsSmall enough for positioning on a shelf, but with sufficient sonic scale to fill a medium-sized room.
You want options beyond ‘back’ and ‘white’There are a trio of more interesting options available.
You admire interesting engineeringThe titanium dome tweeter time-aligned in the throat of the IsoFlare mid/bass driver counts as ‘interesting’ for sure.
You’re using them on a desktopNot only are they slightly large for this purpose, the ‘power on’ hissing gets quite old quite quickly…
You like to know what’s going onHow much volume have you dialled in? Which input has been selected? Who can say?
You’re sensitive to the quality of control interfacesFyne Audio always knew the remote control handset would be the only way to interact with the Cubitt 5 — so why did it specify the cheapest it could find?
The Cambridge L/R S system handily undercuts the Fyne Audio on price, and has an awful lot to recommend it both aesthetically and in audio terms. Not quite the room-filling ability of the Cubitt 5, mind you.
And the same is true of the Ruark Audio MR1 mk3, but if you want a well-specified and great-sounding system that’s genuinely small enough for desktop use and isn’t as pricey as the Fyne Audio, you need look no further.
Almost all my testing is done with the Cubitt 5 positioned on a pair of Soundstyle S2 speaker stands, joined together by their supplied connecting cable and with the primary speaker plugged into the mains.
Sources include a FiiO M15S digital audio player with aptX HD Bluetooth capability, a Technics SL-1300G with Goldring 1022 cartridge for vinyl via the phono stage, a Rega Apollo CD player connected to the optical input, and a Philips OLED806 via the HDMI socket.
Which means a lot of different content, stored in a variety of file types, and of many resolutions, is available — and then I listen for almost an entire working week.

29,2026-10-24,"Movie night with streaming services can be frustrating. Sometimes the apps don't even have the film you want to watch, and then when they do, you pay $4 to $6 a pop to rent it.
When you actually watch that movie, whether it was on the service or not, it ends up as a compressed, low-bitrate digital file. Most people believe physical media is dead, but secondhand shops, garage sales, and pawn shops are flooded with Blu-rays that people bought once and then dumped for pennies.
Dropping a flat $200 budget entirely on thrifted 4K discs, ripping them with MakeMKV to a local home server, and feeding an uncompressed Plex library felt like a much better deal than paying for streaming services. When you factor in the true cost of recurring digital rentals, loss of ownership, and the abysmal bitrates of streaming services, thrift hunting physical media isn't a dying hobby. It's the ultimate financial and technical hack for building an elite personal streaming empire.
When you stream movies from services like Netflix, Prime Video, or Apple TV, you get a bit-rate illusion. It might say the video you're watching is in 4K, but in reality, it is a compressed lie compared to the quality of your physical discs.
Commercial streaming platforms compress 4K video to 15Mbps to 25Mbps to save bandwidth and make it accessible to people without superfast internet speeds. As a result, you get crushing macroblocks in dark scenes, muddy gradients, and compressed audio. It just never matches the uncompressed reality you get on physical discs.
A 4K Blu-ray disc boasts bit rates soaring anywhere from 60Mbps to over 100Mbps. Paired with lossless Dolby TrueHD, DTS-HD Master Audio, or Dolby Atmos, it feels and sounds significantly different when you're watching it. Ripping that disc directly into a Plex remux gives you absolute reference-grade quality that streaming services cannot touch.
It's a home-labbing cinephile's dream
 Thrifted discs essentially destroy digital rentals on cost. You can easily build a Blu-ray library for $150. It might sound far-fetched when you think about a brand-new 4K Blu-ray retailing for about $25 to $30, depending on the movie. But by scouring thrift stores, pawn shops, and clearance bins, you can routinely pick up 4K discs for $5 to $8 apiece.
The secondhand market is absolutely fantastic for this. This lets you source anywhere from 20 to 30 top-tier films for about $200, averaging about $6 per movie (roughly the same as a single modern digital rental that gives you access for 48 hours). When that digital rental vanishes after 48 hours, you lose access and have to rent it again, whereas a thrifted physical disc is a permanent asset.
Ripping that disc into Plex means it's yours forever. If you want to watch or rewatch that movie twice or share it with family members on your Plex server, the per-view cost drops even lower and lower. It pays for itself almost instantly compared to continuous digital rentals.
 If you want to build up your own physical media library, then here's how to get started. First, hunt smart. Target local thrift chains, pawn shops, and Facebook Marketplace lots rather than paying retail collector prices. Sometimes all it takes is a quick glance over a sale bin to find a hidden gem. When you pick up your Blu-ray discs, especially secondhand ones, double-check the condition. Sometimes there are deep scratches, and while 4K Blu-ray discs are remarkably resilient because of their hard-coat polymer layers, they're not always perfect.
After you find the physical discs you want to rip, you'll need an Ultra HD-friendly drive. A range of external Blu-ray drives is available on the market, but make sure you pick one compatible with 4K. Be sure to double-check the specifications. You can flash it with custom firmware to support UHD reading and then hook it up to your home server or desktop. Blu-ray drives can be an expensive upfront purchase, costing around $100, but once you buy one, you can rip as many Blu-rays as you want for the foreseeable future.
To rip the film itself, you can use MakeMKV. You can download this software, and it lets you extract the main movie file as an uncompressed MKV container, preserving the original video track and lossless audio streams without quality loss. When you do, be sure to select only the audio tracks you need instead of all the languages included on the disc. This will help you save space.
And lastly, it's time to feed your Plex library. Drop the files into your structured Plex media directories, let the scanner pull metadata, and enjoy pristine 4K streaming anywhere in your home.
The digital rental economy relies on making you pay repeatedly for temporary access to compressed files, or paying a subscription for services that constantly remove your favorite movies. Spending $150 on thrifted physical media proves you can build a high-performance local streaming library that outclasses commercial streaming providers while keeping full ownership of your data.
Stop throwing money away on temporary digital rentals. Hit up your local secondhand shops and start building a Plex library that you actually own.
All Blu-ray drives aren't created equal when you're backing up 4K movies for self-hosted media servers.

31,2026-10-24,"Nokia might not be the name you think of for phones these days, but its best phones of all time are still iconic today, especially for millennials and Gen Xers. In recent years, the brand has tried to build off of its former glory days with contemporary remixes of classics like the 8210 or the more famous 3310. Sadly, both the Nokia label and its spiritual torch bearer have shrunk back from the global stage. They're still around, but if you want one, you'll need to go to extra lengths.
HMD Global, producer of Nokia-branded phones since 2016, went to market with six retro-style feature phones this summer: the Nokia 200 4G, the 210 4G, the 215 4G 2nd Edition, the 235 4G 2nd Edition, the 123 Shield, and the 300 Charge. The company last released a Nokia smartphone back in September 2023 with the Nokia C210; as of writing, it maintains software support for only one Android device. HMD, which stands for Human Mobile Devices, reportedly extended a brand licensing deal with the Finnish telecom equipment vendor that will keep Nokia in its portfolio until around 2029.
Nokia sold its consumer devices division to Microsoft in 2014 as part of the latter's bid to enhance the Windows Phone ecosystem. The same unit was effectively written off in 2015 and was salvaged the next year when former Nokia executives formed HMD Global to buy it. Granted, the question of who owns Nokia goes deeper than that, but HMD certainly shares lineage with the company as it once was. The start-up spent several years releasing Nokia-branded Android smartphones across the price spectrum along with the occasional feature phone redux, usually as part of a promotional tie-in (such as with the 2021 James Bond film ""No Time to Die,"" which featured a new Nokia 3310).
In 2024, the company began producing smartphones and feature phones with the HMD label for the first time. The company ended device sales in the United States in 2025, likely due in part to uncertainties around tariffs at the time. The company is currently focused on low-end phones for markets such as India and Thailand, including ""hybrid"" devices that combine feature phone form factors with access to artificial intelligence features. It's a development that doesn't bode well for any return to America, where premium phones rule the roost. Then again, if Dutch Android phone maker Fairphone can come to the U.S. for the first time ever in 2026, why can't Nokia?"
32,2026-10-24,"Cisco (CSCO), Wells Fargo (WFC) and four other companies have opted out of plans by crypto exchange OKX and New York Stock Exchange parent Intercontinental Exchange (ICE) to offer blockchain-based trading of their shares, bringing the total objections to 22 as issuers weigh participation in the SEC’s new framework for tokenized stocks.
The companies, along with Arm Holdings (ARM), Moderna (MRNA), Intuit (INTU) and Marvell Technology (MRVL), were named in an updated public notice filed Friday by OKXICE, the two firms’ joint venture.
The additions extend a list that already included some of the world’s largest publicly traded companies, such as Microsoft MSFT$524.32·Market Closed, Alphabet (GOOG, GOOGL), Goldman Sachs (GS) and McDonald’s and said no to the venue’s plan in recent weeks.
The growing number of objections signals a hurdle for a new market structure that would let U.S. investors trade stocks around the clock on blockchains. The SEC’s five-year “Innovation Exemption” allows qualifying tokenized securities venues, or TSVs, to test trading through automated market makers — software that executes trades using pools of assets — while giving issuers a say in whether their shares participate.
OKXICE, backed by one of the world's biggest exchange operators, issued its initial public notice on Oct. 4, outlining plans to use Uniswap (UNI) liquidity pools on OKX’s XLayer blockchain, with transactions settled in stablecoins. Venues must notify issuers and give them 30 days to object before offering tokenized versions of their shares.
The venture’s original proposed trading roster covered 62 companies. Wells Fargo and ARM were not among them, while Cisco, Moderna, Intuit and Marvell were included.
That means the 22 objections cannot all be counted as departures from the original selection. Cerebras Systems, whose objection was disclosed earlier, was also outside that roster. An objection blocks trading on the notifying venue but does not establish opposition to every form of stock tokenization.
Andrew Cuomo, OKXICE’s co-chairman and former New York governor, said earlier this week that such responses were expected.
“We always anticipated that, because this is such a dramatic and revolutionary change, many companies would want to wait and see how it goes rather than be the first to participate,” Cuomo said during a Monday press call.
Limits on participating companies and trading volumes make the framework “a sandbox — a model demonstration,” he said.

33,2026-10-24,"This is an excerpt from CoinDesk's new ‘Tokenization Weekly’ newsletter that will be launching soon. Sign-up details will be here when it goes live.
The SEC's new framework for tokenized stock trading looked, at first glance, roughly a month ago, like a fast track to putting U.S. equities on crypto rails.
The first real-world test hasn't looked particularly encouraging so far.
A growing group of public companies has objected to having their shares traded on the tokenized-securities venue being built by OKX and Intercontinental Exchange (ICE), the owner of the New York Stock Exchange.
This week’s of objections included some of the biggest names yet: Microsoft (MSFT), Alphabet (GOOG, GOOGL), Goldman Sachs (GS) McDonalds (MCD) and three others on Monday, then Wells Fargo (WFC) and Cisco (CSCO) and four more firms on Friday rejected having tokenized versions of their shares traded on OKXICE, bringing the disclosed total now to 22 objecting issuers.
Seeing some of the world's largest companies say no may not exactly be the start that tokenization advocates had hoped for.
But it also isn't necessarily a major blow to the SEC's experiment.
Read more: OKX-ICE co-chair Andrew Cuomo calls corporate objections a ‘natural response’ to tokenized stock plans
Before trading in a company's shares, a venue must notify the issuer and give the issuer 30 days to object. If the company stays silent, trading can begin.
That gives public companies a say in whether unaffiliated third parties tokenize and trade their shares, but it also tilts the decision toward caution.
Global law firm Latham & Watkins has warned issuers that silence effectively becomes an irrevocable decision: the SEC order provides no mechanism to object after the 30-day window or withdraw a prior non-objection once trading begins.
So when a legal or investor-relations team receives notice about a new market structure it may know little about, saying no while it figures things out can be the rational choice.
“Most of these companies had no idea a third party had tokenized their stock until the OKXICE notice went up,” Dave Hendricks, CEO and founder of tokenization firm Vertalo, told CoinDesk.
“Once they do, the questions come fast: who holds the shares behind the token, how do dividends and votes reach those holders, what does the transfer agent see?” he added.
If those questions can't be answered within 30 days, he said, objecting makes sense.
Olivia Vande Woude, head of tokenization at Ava Labs, also cautioned against reading the growing list as a verdict on OKXICE.
“It's the first venue to send notices under the SEC's Innovation Exemption, so it's the first to get the answers,” she said.
And because objecting costs nothing while silence counts as consent, she expects the list to keep growing.
“Given how easy the opt-out is, the list of objectors is likely to keep growing, and the next venue that sends notices should expect similar resistance,” Vande Woude said.
That may be less a sign that the experiment is failing than an indication of what the experiment is uncovering.
“This exemption is a data-gathering exercise,” Vande Woude said. “What it's showing is that public companies want a say in how their shares come onchain.”
Nasdaq offers a useful example of what the objections do – and don't – mean.
The exchange operator has been a vocal supporter of tokenization and is pursuing its own model for tokenized securities. But Nasdaq Inc., whose shares trade under NDAQ, said it intends to object to having tokenized versions of its own stock traded on TSVs under the current exemption.
That reflects a disagreement over market structure, not tokenization itself.
Nasdaq warned that TSVs operating outside the National Market System (NMS) could fragment liquidity, weaken price discovery and lack safeguards such as real-time best-price display and consolidated-tape reporting. Its own model keeps tokenized securities inside the existing exchange framework.
There is also a simpler question for issuers: what's in it for them?
Reid Noch, vice president of U.S. equity market structure at TD Securities, told CoinDesk the bigger issue is that the upside for issuers remains difficult to quantify. Tokenization could bring additional demand for U.S. shares, particularly from overseas investors, but the SEC framework may not generate enough new demand to offset the risks of thin, 24/7 trading outside the familiar market structure.
That doesn't mean everyone is saying no.
Securitize (SECZ), which specializes in tokenized securities, told CoinDesk it does not plan to object to inclusion on the OKX-ICE venue. A representative of digital asset custodian BitGo (BTGO) also said in an email that the company won't object to the listing.
Both have an obvious interest in helping the tokenized-asset market grow, but Securitize's response also pointed to what may be needed for more traditional companies to get comfortable.
“There's an important education process underway,” CEO Carlos Domingo said.
The firm said it has been reaching out to issuers that are or may become involved with its own tokenized stocks, including through webinars explaining the different trading models, shareholder rights and potential trade-offs.
That probably offers the clearest glimpse of what comes next: Education and time.
The SEC may have removed one regulatory barrier to tokenized stock trading. Getting public companies comfortable enough to use it could take considerably longer.

36,2026-10-24,"It actually seems like an episode of Black Mirror that it’s legal for a company to buy and sell our personal data without our consent, but that’s the reality in the US. Apple has built some really useful tools to limit future tracking, including App Tracking Transparency, Hide My Email, and iCloud Private Relay, but this doesn’t solve your actual problem, though. What they can’t do is remove the data that has already been collected, bought, and spread across hundreds of broker databases over the past decade.
Most people are shocked when they learn just how much information marketers and scammers can find out about them by purchasing it from one of many data brokers. Those long terms and conditions you agree to (but didn’t read) when you download a new app or register with a new website often include simple clauses granting permission to “share data with selected partners.” What that actually means is that data as sensitive as your location history can be sold to data brokers, who then resell it to anyone willing to pay for it.
Data brokers collect personal data from multiple sources to build detailed profiles of individuals. Your cell phone number, home address, Social Security numbers, maiden name, family connections, past employers, car loans, and previous addresses are packaged and sold to marketers and anyone else who wants them (including people who will use them for nefarious purposes). The industry is largely unregulated, which means there are very few guardrails and protections on who can buy this information or what they can do with it.
The security risk absolutely compounds over time. Scammers and hackers who already know your job title, your neighbors’ names, and where you grew up can craft phishing messages that are really hard to spot. For anyone concerned about physical safety, people search sites serve as public, searchable directories that can map your location, your relatives, and your daily patterns to anyone looking.
In most US states, you have a legal right to demand that your information be removed from these databases. Unfortunately, doing so is incredibly time-consuming and deliberately difficult. You have to contact literally hundreds of companies, each of which makes the process difficult on purpose to discourage people from trying. Opt-out forms can be hard to find on a website, the process sometimes requires lengthy application forms, and you cannot make a single request on behalf of your entire family. Each family member must apply separately to have their data removed.
Even if you succeed, there’s nothing to stop brokers from purchasing new data on you and starting the process over again. Doing this properly across hundreds of sites is a part-time job, and the success rate without persistent follow-up is poor. Frankly, none of this should be legal. 
Incogni automates this entire process for you, so you can focus on living your life rather than trying to protect your personal data. Once you set up your account with Incogni, it sends legally binding removal requests to 420+ data broker databases on your behalf and handles the follow-up when brokers push back or attempt to re-collect your data. Incogni doesn’t do this once and stop. It keeps repeating the process at regular intervals to ensure your information stays off these databases, rather than being removed a single time.
Incogni’s Unlimited plan extends coverage to 3,000+ additional sites and lets you submit custom removal requests for any site on which you find your information on. You get a dashboard that tracks the status of every request in real time, so you can see what has been removed and what is still in progress. The end result is fewer robocalls, less spam, and a much smaller target on your back for identity thieves and scammers. You get all this without spending hours each week fighting with data brokers.
This week is Cybersecurity Awareness Week, an annual campaign that reminds individuals and companies to strengthen their digital security. The basics are always about strong passwords, multi-factor authentication, and recognizing phishing emails. There’s a threat that doesn’t get enough attention during this week: your personal data and your family’s personal data that’s already out there in broker databases, and it brings just as much of a risk as clicking a phishing link. Removing it from the market is one of the most practical safety steps you can take this week to improve your physical and digital safety.
Use promo code 9TO5MAC at checkout for 58% off. You can cancel at any time. Get started today and start protecting your family’s personal data. If there’s ever a week to deal with this, it’s this one.

37,2026-10-24,"Apple @ Work is exclusively brought to you by Mosyle, the only Apple Unified Platform. Mosyle is the only solution that integrates in a single professional grade platform all the solutions necessary to seamlessly and automatically deploy, manage, and protect Apple devices at work. Over 45,000 organizations trust Mosyle to make millions of Apple devices work ready with no effort and at an affordable cost. Request your EXTENDED TRIAL today and understand why Mosyle is everything you need to work with Apple.
Most IT teams have a reasonable handle on what AI apps are installed across their Mac environment. Your device management vendor can show you what’s running; policies can block which apps shouldn’t be there; and vendors have been adding AI visibility features over the past year. The app layer is mostly solved, but the browser remains a concern depending on your environment.
About Apple @ Work: Bradley Chambers has been an Apple IT admin since 2009. Through his experience deploying and managing firewalls, switches, a mobile device management system, enterprise grade WiFi, 1000s of Macs, and 1000s of iPads, Bradley will highlight ways in which Apple IT managers deploy Apple devices, build networks to support them, train users, share stories from the trenches of IT management, and ways Apple could improve its products for IT departments.
When AI governance topic comes up, it’s usually about:
Those are all actual questions that IT teams should know, and if they don’t, then that is priority one. If an employee installs an unapproved AI coding tool or a local model inference engine, IT can detect it, flag it, and automatically remove it. That part of the problem is largely within reach for any organization running a managed Mac fleet.
Here’s the bigger issue for most IT teams: an employee opens Safari or Chrome, goes to ChatGPT or Claude, logs into their free personal account, and uploads a corporate document to ask the model a question about it. No app was installed, and nothing shows up in your app inventory report from your device management vendor. The action happens entirely inside a browser that IT typically has no visibility into at the web application layer, depending on your filtering setup.
Jamf is addressing this directly with browser AI governance coming in Q4 2026. The capability will let admins identify the use of personal AI accounts in Safari, Chrome, Firefox, Edge, and other browsers, and enforce policies at the point of action, including blocking actions. I am very interested to see this in action once it arrives.
A different answer to the same problem is the enterprise browser, and I think many orgs will consider this going forward. Island is the most prominent example in this space that I’ve seen. The idea is that instead of trying to bolt governance into an existing browser from the outside that’s not necessarily native, you give employees a managed browser that IT controls from the ground up. Every use case happens inside a container IT governs, including which web AI tools employees can access, whether they can paste corporate data into a prompt, and what can be downloaded or uploaded to it.
The tradeoff is there, though. Asking employees to switch their primary browser is a harder ask than deploying a policy through an extension, and on Mac specifically, Safari is deeply integrated in ways that make replacing it as their daily browser likely to meet pushback. Chrome runs into the same problem. Island is growing rapidly, though. They recently announced a $400 million Series F round at a $ 6.4 billion valuation, and I think their model might end up being the default for many enterprises.
Ultimately, the tools to manage this are either just arriving or still in development; the policy frameworks haven’t caught up with the coming technical capabilities, and use cases change quickly.
AI governance on the Mac is not just about managing which AI apps are installed. It’s much harder than that. The browser is where corporate data actually moves to personal AI accounts, and it’s the part of the stack that traditional Apple management tools were never designed to govern at the web application layer. I expect this market to evolve a lot in the coming years.

38,2026-10-24,"Sudan is suffering from a cost-of-living crisis affecting millions across the country.
Save
Share

Azhari Mohamed drives his minibus and eight passengers seven times a day from the Shagar neighbourhood in the Port of Sudan to the city centre and back again.
But despite the long hours – working from 6am to 9pm – fuel shortages in the city have made it difficult for the 60-year-old to make a living.
Azhari’s minibus uses three gallons (11 litres) of petrol a day, costing him about 105,000 Sudanese pounds ($11.30 a day, according to Sudan’s parallel-market exchange rate). With fuel increasingly difficult to obtain, he now spends two days on the road and a third day queuing for fuel.
He can spend more than five hours waiting in line when he needs to fill the tank of his minibus and often finds petrol has run out before his turn comes. When that happens, he goes to the back of the queue at another petrol station hoping to fill up there.
The shortages have led to price rises with a gallon (3.8 litres) of petrol now costing 40,000 Sudanese pounds ($4.30) in Gazira State, south of Khartoum, while in Port Sudan it is 35,000 pounds ($3.80) a gallon.


“We can no longer work because of the fuel shortages. The transport fares are no longer enough to cover the cost of running the vehicle and our household expenses,” Azhari told Al Jazeera.
He says the long hours spent queueing for petrol is time he could spend earning money to support his family.


“I spend long hours of the day waiting to refuel, so I have to work extra hours at night to make up for the time I lose during the day,” he said. “My livelihood depends on what I earn each day.”

After subtracting his vehicle’s operating costs, Azhari has just 50,000 Sudanese pounds ($5.40) left a day, barely enough to cover the needs of his family, which includes his wife and daughter who is at university and needs support for her tuition fees and daily expenses.
“We just need fuel to be available so we can work,” he said. “If fuel is available, we will not face this obstacle.”





















Due to the crisis, Sudan’s Transport Chambers Union, which regulates public and private transport services, this month raised intercity bus fares by 40% and minibus fares by 30%.
Aziza Ibrahim from the Umm al-Qura neighbourhood of Port Sudan, works as a cleaner to support her four children. Just getting to work by bus has become a huge financial burden for the 38-year-old, who has cut back her work days to three times a week due to the high travel costs.

Before the war, a journey from her home to the main transport station in the city centre cost 700 Sudanese pounds ($0.08) per passenger. Fares have since risen to 2,500 pounds ($0.27) on some routes, while others charge 2,000 pounds ($0.22).

“I go to work three days a week to reduce transport costs. I need 11,000 pounds ($1.18) a day for the round trip between my home and workplace,” she told Al Jazeera.

Transport costs now consume nearly half of Aziza’s monthly salary of 400,000 pounds ($43), while costly treatment for her diabetes is another financial burden.

“Sometimes I have to borrow money just to be able to go to work. I repay it at the beginning of the month when I receive my salary, which is barely enough to cover my family’s needs,” she told Al Jazeera.

According to the United Nations Development Programme, the war has pushed average incomes in Sudan back to levels last seen in the 1990s. 
That is partly due to the effects of the civil war on Sudan’s oil sector. Fighting between the Sudanese Armed Forces (SAF) and rival paramilitary Rapid Support Forces (RSF) began on April 15, 2023.
Last year, the Ministry of Energy and Petroleum estimated war-related losses at $20 billion. The World Bank has reported that attacks on the Balila oil field disrupted supplies to the Khartoum refinery, which previously met about 60% of the country’s demand for petrol and liquefied petroleum gas.
War damage has seen a sharp decrease in oil production, leaving Sudan reliant on imports amid the global fuel crisis that has pushed up prices of gas, petrol and diesel, among other commodities.
Economic analyst Sidi Ohaj said that the government requirements for petroleum importers have also contributed to the shortages. They include a stipulation that importers must deposit 200kg of gold in the Central Bank and own fuel storage facilities in order to operate in Sudan. Some importers have been unable to meet those requirements, forcing them out of the market which reduces supply.




“One of the causes of the current crisis is the government’s decision to regulate the operations of petroleum import companies,” Sidi told Al Jazeera. “Some companies did not meet the requirements, which led to their exit from the market. This reduced supply relative to demand.”  

The government needs to urgently ease import procedures to increase domestic supplies, he said, with shortages affecting the wider economy.
But as the war between the United States and Iran continues to disrupt global energy markets, there is little hope the situation will improve any time soon.

“The continuation of the war [in Sudan], particularly drone attacks that threaten infrastructure across Sudan, is one of the main challenges facing the oil sector,” Sidi told Al Jazeera.
 

 

Prices have also risen due to the energy crisis, impacting the purchasing power of citizens and profits of businesses.
Ahmed al-Haj is a local trader in Omdurman market who sells skincare products, such as petroleum jelly, which he packages himself . He said high energy prices are costing almost all areas of his business.
“The raw materials for the products I sell are imported. Higher diesel and petrol prices have increased the cost of transporting a tonne of goods by 30 to 50 percent,” Ahmed told Al Jazeera.
“That increase is then reflected in the price of the products.”
When electricity cuts force him to use a fuel-powered generator for heating or packaging, the additional fuel costs add to his daily bills.
Deliveries within Omdurman, which previously cost 5,000 Sudanese pounds ($0.70) now cost between 10,000 and 12,000 pounds ($1.07 and $1.29). Reaching customers in other states has become impossible due to the high transport costs.
“Transport and distribution workers have also reduced the number of trips they make, leading to goods piling up at traders’ premises,” he added.

40,2026-10-24,"Android is all about choice and customization, but to some, that identity is fading away. Every version of Android that ships with a commercial phone is a far cry from the basic Android Open Source Project release, complete with custom manufacturer tweaks — and restrictions. Pixel phones arrive with Google's vision for Android, sometimes mistakenly referred to as ""stock Android."" In many ways, it's more limited than the Android skins you get from Samsung or Motorola. 
I've written about one way Pixel phones are limited by software: the Battery Health Assistance feature you can't turn off. It's designed to preserve the long-term health of your battery by throttling voltage and charging speed. However, there's a flip side to Battery Health Assistance. If you have an older Pixel, Battery Health Assistance is enabled by default, but you can manually disable it.
I pulled my Google Pixel 8a review unit out of the drawer, fired it up for the first time in over a year, and I'm here to walk you through how to reclaim your older Pixel's battery life and charging speed. 
Your Pixel can use AI to analyze your usage.
Before you go ahead and turn off Battery Health Assistance, you should know what it does. Battery Health Assistance debuted on the Google Pixel 9a, a midrange Android phone released in 2025. At the time, Google said that ""Pixel software will manage battery performance to help maintain battery health as your battery ages."" Later, we learned the full extent of Battery Health Assistance in a support document: 
As your battery ages, you may notice small decreases in your battery’s runtime. Based on adjusted capacity, Battery Health Assistance also tunes the phone’s charging speed. You may notice a slight change in battery charging performance. 
The simple version is that Google is proactively limiting your battery's performance in an effort to preserve its long-term health. It's doing so by decreasing the battery's maximum voltage in stages, which in turn reduces relative battery life and peak charging speeds. It happens gradually, beginning at 200 cycles and continuing through 1,000 cycles.
This might be preferred if you're the kind of person who keeps their phone until it breaks, or through the modern Pixel's seven-year software support window. On the other hand, those who replace their phone every few years might sacrifice battery performance without reaping the benefits. 
Battery Health Assistance launched with the Pixel 9a, and has shipped with every new Pixel phone since. On these devices, Battery Health Assistance can't be turned off. It came to the Pixel 6a, Pixel 7 series, Pixel 8 series, and Pixel 9 series (excluding Pixel 9a) in a software update. Following the Android 16 update, a server-side push made Battery Health Assistance available on these phones. 
There's a small group of Pixel phones that have Battery Health Assistance and can turn it off, so here's how to do it. 
To make things clear, owners of the following Pixel phones can disable Battery Health Assistance: Pixel 6a, Pixel 7, Pixel 7 Pro, Pixel 7a, Pixel 8, Pixel 8 Pro, Pixel 8a, Pixel 9, Pixel 9 Pro, Pixel 9 Pro XL, and Pixel 9 Pro Fold. If you have one of these models, you'll see a new Battery Health page in Settings following the Android 16 update. Since we're now on Android 17, you've probably updated to Android 16 already.
I turned off the feature by navigating to Settings → Battery → Battery Health. Here, you'll see your phone's relative battery capacity, AI-powered battery health tips, and your Charging Optimization preference. At the very bottom, you'll find Battery Health Assistance — that's the one you should tap. 
This opens a page that explains Battery Health Assistance. There's a toggle beside Use Battery Health Assistance that is enabled by default, but you can tap it to flip it off. Of course, you can also choose to leave it on and maximize the lifespan of your battery. It's easy enough if you have the right Pixel model, but remember, this toggle isn't available on the Pixel 9a and newer.
While you're in the Battery Health menu, you can tweak the Charging Optimization mode. Luckily, every Pixel owner can modify this setting if their device has it. You can disable it entirely, use Adaptive Charging to finish charging when you usually unplug, or use Limit to 80% to set a hard charging cap.
Batteries are consumable components, and they degrade over time. Features like Battery Health Assistance are designed to soften the blow and prolong battery health.
That said, there are other options. Rather than use software-based battery throttling, you could budget for battery replacements and plan for a swap every few years. If you're the kind of person that trades in your phone early and often, you'll likely never see the major effects of battery degradation that Battery Health Assistance is designed to avoid.
That's why all Pixels should have the option to use Battery Health Assistance. I don't blame Google for enabling it by default, but users should be able to choose to turn it off if they feel strongly about battery throttling. If you're the owner of a slightly older Pixel, you get the best of both worlds. You have access to the feature if you want it, but aren't locked into it. 
We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.
