Thereafter
 
$4.691 billion



That leaves out interest and the cash they need to run and improve the airline.  About 67% of their assets at book value are mortgaged, and it’s not clear how much they can raise against the rest.  They claim $6 billion in unencumbered assets but they surely can’t raise another $6 billion, and their financing costs are likely to only go up. 
And there are severe restrictions in their mortgage on the frequent flyer program including at least $750 million of quarter-end liquidity and a maximum senior debt to collateral ratio of 62.5%. Failing either test would divert credit card revenue to accelerate repayment of debt.    



Credit card processors could also hold back revenue from ticket sales as a hedge against continued operations, which only serves to accelerate any cash crunch.  We’ll learn more with JetBlue’s third quarter earnings call on Tuesday. 
JetBlue’s Turnaround Is A Race Against Time
JetBlue is concentrating on their East Coast leisure network, says they’re improving reliability, adding premium products to boost revenue (while further degrading standard coach) and trying to earn more their credit cards with lounges and updates to the frequent flyer program. 
Second quarter earnings showed higher revenue, though slightly lagging the industry, but real improved cash from Barclays with new premium card acquisitions up almost 40% (as you’d expect – it didn’t make sense to apply for the high annual fee credit card whose primary benefit was lounge access before there were any lounges to access). Fort Lauderdale revenue per available seat mile increased 11% despite nearly 40% more capacity.
They still had a $141 million operating loss in the second quarter, so they wouldn’t have made money even if interest expense was zero.  The cost of fuel is up, but JetBlue’s problems long predate that.
 



United May Want JetBlue In Bankruptcy
On United’s Wednesday earnings call, Jamie Baker asked Scott Kirby why United shouldn’t be a potential solution for JetBlue as it considers restructuring. Kirby replied:
So I’m not going to create any new news. I just have nothing new to add on that.
Kirby, who opines on almost anything – including wanting a national wealth tax and near-100% inheritance tax – simply moved on, refusing comment.  

I wrote in May that a United acquisition would make sense only in the context of a controlled bankruptcy.  Kirby had said that JetBlue’s losses were too large and that they’d need an improvement of about 25 percentage points in its margins to make an acquisition work.
JetBlue’s market cap is now less than $1 billion.  There’d be an acquisition premium, but buying the shares cheap doesn’t remove the debt which approaches $9 billion.
Combining the airlines also means integration costs and bringing employees onto United’s higher pay scales. 
United would value New York JFK, JetBlue’s position in Boston, and a South Florida hub.  It already gets some benefit from its JetBlue partnership.  And they’re well positioned to buy assets to provide liquidity.  
Most of JetBlue’s assets are pledged, but shedding some of those that United is less interested in or cramming down obligations against them, could make them a more attractive target.  United doesn’t necessarily need the planes!

More From View from the Wing

 13 Comments 

Gary Leff is one of the foremost experts in the field of miles, points, and frequent business travel - a topic he has covered since 2002. 

Co-founder of frequent flyer community InsideFlyer.com, emcee of the Freddie Awards, and named one of the ""World's Top Travel Experts"" by Conde' Nast Traveler (2010-Present) Gary has been a guest on most major news media, profiled in several top print publications, and published broadly on the topic of consumer loyalty. More About Gary »
More articles by Gary Leff »
Gary
When you had your  list pf the worst airline executives I commented that you were far too kind to Robin Hayes. He should have been second only to Doug Parker. I reiterate that position.
There is no room at airports for so many aircraft .
Other than change in administration, seems funny that regulators would reject AA – Jetblue Alliance but approve UA takeover of Jetblue slots and gates at JFK and Boston, respectively.
There is room at all airports for a solid hard product ,strong customer service and a loyalty program that most frequent flyers want to be engaged in where  the currency is considered valuable.Failure to differentiate leads to only to bloody fare wars and a highly commoditized product where mostly all brands and experiences are the same.
Less room throughout coach eliminates JetBlue’s biggest differentiator. 
I wish the old JetBlue could have succeeded.
I remember my first JetBlue flight and thinking how novel is was to have free wifi.
I have no idea how concentrated those bonds are, but it’s not uncommon for advisors to get out in front and “hire themselves” by getting a small handful of creditors together so that now they represent “a group”.  And then they let the press know about it and there’s a story out there that these are the advisors representing the group.
The real question is what percentage of the issuance this group represents, and what the debt docs for that tranche say in terms of covenants and the like that can allow the bondholders to exert pressure, etc.  And different creditors want different things (some might be happy to exchange debt for equity and own the company, for others depending on their fund structure the idea of taking back equity would be a non-starter.  Much will depend on whose hands the debt is in when the music starts to stop.
That said, it’s been clear for some time now what direction B6 is headed in.  Even without dramatically increased fuel prices a reorg would have been challenging.  But with operational costs and expenses being what they are for B6 right now… not going to be an easy road.
I won’t accept anything other than Chapter 7 and complete destruction.   As Alfred Jarry put it, even the ruins must be destroyed.
I fly out of Boston, and used to use JetBlue a lot. It was, honestly, my go-to airline for doing any domestic flight, except to the West Coast. Then, what started happening was LONG delays (we sat in New Orleans once for over 6 hours as they kept delaying for reasons that were never explained) on just about everything, OR they started just cancelling flights leaving me to scramble to find an alternative. I stopped flying them to Florida completely, as AA has better schedules, and we’re leaving for San Juan an a few weeks and changed to Delta because they cancelled the flight AGAIN. These are self-inflicted wounds because, again, I’m totally NOT loyal to airlines but I was MOST loyal to JetBlue and always checked them first. So if Boston was supposed to be one of their key airports, they failed, and have become unreliable and also strangely unavailable.
I can’t imagine a world where JFK isn’t divided up, whether United acquires them or not. There’s no way UA will take JFK in its entirety.
When the tide goes out… the macro-level bad policies and corruption, unfortunately, is making matters worse for jetBlue (and American, and every company/person out there, but for a few. Say your silly ‘TDS,’ if you want. Won’t change the reality that new forever-wars, unnecessary tariffs, attacks on consumer and worker protections, all leads to bad outcomes for most people.)
That said, they still have war-chest of cash, slots at JFK, gates at BOS, and a modern fleet. Would much rather have an independent B6 than it become part of UA. Wishing them well.
There is no way United gets international out of JFK and EWR. EWR is massive as it is and marketed as a NYC airport. United doesn’t need JetBlue.
Since UA burned $2 billion in cash in the 3rd quarter and is now the most indebted US airline, the notion that UA can pull out a checkbook – even if the feds allowed it (which they won’t) is beyond silly
JFK will be carved up or the feds will just retire slots that B6 won’t use but no single entity will get any of B6′ assets at any airport.

85,2026-10-24,"Get quick access to your favorite articles

Antonelli pole shows Mercedes is 'finally seeing the results' of car upgrades
Leclerc and Hamilton's differing theories behind Ferrari's Austin qualifying disappointment

F1 United States GP: Ferrari completes perfect practice record as Hamilton tops FP3
James Hunt’s 10 greatest races
F1 United States GP live commentary and updates - Hamilton leads FP3
F1 United States GP live commentary and updates - Antonelli on pole
Valtteri Bottas, Cadillac Racing, Sergio Perez, Cadillac Racing
Photo by: Mark Sutton / Formula 1 via Getty Images
The US GP weekend naturally means the spotlight gravitates towards Formula 1’s two American-owned teams. That has led to some awkward questions for Cadillac amid ongoing speculation over its driver line-up for 2027 and beyond.
Both Sergio Perez and Valtteri Bottas are on “multi-year” contracts, understood to be a so-called one-plus-one arrangement in which the second year is optional, subject to either party fulfilling various conditions. Such has been the disparity in performance between the two drivers that Bottas’s position has been the object of paddock gossip for months.
Recently appointed team principal Marcin Budkowski has already had to do some firefighting on this, as had his predecessor Graeme Lowdon. But a rather peculiar moment in the FIA’s press conference for team representatives on Friday in Austin suggested Cadillac’s plans aren’t as fixed as previously billed.
The catalyst was a soft question from one of the broadcasters on the theme of Perez’s upcoming 300th grand prix start: “How difficult is it to evaluate a driver who’s fighting at the back of the grid, at the same time as the impression is that he’s having a better season than his team-mate? And quickly on drivers, would you be interested in having any conversation with Esteban [Ocon] regarding maybe a reserve role?”
In trying to give a suitably anodyne non-answer, Budkowski began to flounder.
“Look, for a driver like Checo, who, as you said, has a huge amount of experience and a number of races, I can tell you he doesn’t behave or drive like a driver who is anywhere close to retirement,” he said.
“He’s pushing very hard behind the wheel. He’s pushing us very hard also behind the scenes, in the debrief rooms and outside, and regularly texting me and other members of the team to push us to improve. He’s hungry, he’s motivated, and that’s actually really good to see. He does it in his own style.
Marcin Budkowski, Cadillac Team Principal
Photo by: Alex Bierens de Haan / LAT Images via Getty Images
“We have two drivers: one is more hot and one is probably more cold, if we could say, from there. And they both push the team in their own particular ways, but Checo is, yes, pushing us very hard. And just looking at the way his body language on the grid, when he gets his helmet on and he gets in the car, he’s getting ready for a fight. There’s a lot of fire, there’s a lot of motivation there, so it’s really impressive for a driver of this experience.
“And at the same time, we’re getting his feedback and his input in terms of, as an experienced driver. So yes, it’s a very good combination.
“And sorry, the second question was about Esteban Ocon? Well, look, as a team principal, and I’m sure it’s the same with my colleagues, you’re getting calls and contacts from every driver or driver manager that is on the market. So, there are a number of drivers and managers who have contacted me.
“I’m not going to say who it is or who it isn’t, but pretty much all of them that are on the market have been in touch, as you’d expect. But yes, I think it’s the last place here, as you can imagine, where I would communicate anything on driver line-up.”
Were William Shakespeare present, he would no doubt have put quill to parchment and scratched out a note to the effect that the gentleman doth protest too much. Fortunately for the Cadillac team principal, perhaps, Adrian Newey was in the room and acted as a natural magnet for the majority of further questions.
Autosport understands that Ocon is highly unlikely to go to Cadillac, since he and Perez mixed like oil and water when they last coexisted under the same roof. It’s believed he will probably become Aston Martin’s third driver.
Whether or not Cadillac retain Bottas for 2027, it’s known that the team’s owners wish to have an American driver in a race seat as soon as possible. Lowdon’s surprise replacement this summer is an indication of how General Motors and TWG Motorsports’ expectations appear to be running somewhere ahead of the team’s current progress.
Cadillac ran Colton Herta in FP1 at the Circuit of The Americas, but he is a left-field candidate for any race opportunities since he is one point short of qualifying for a superlicence, owing to the FIA having changed the qualifying rubric. If he has meaningful running in another session this year he will qualify – but his rather patchy Formula 2 campaign this season isn’t a very strong calling card.
“Obviously having an American driver in an American team is a great story,” Budkowski told Sky Sports this weekend. “It needs to be the right time and the right driver. But we're working on that. We're working on that with Colton.
“There are some really exciting American talents coming up through the ranks as well. We've seen Ugo Ugochukwu winning F3. We've got [Sebastian] Wheldon coming through to F3 next year.
“So there's a lot of potential here and we'll look into that obviously.”
Let us know what you would like to see from us in the future.
- The Autosport.com Team

86,2026-10-24,"I went to Alabama on behalf of New Atlas to try out Volkswagen’s new Atlas. This play on words came up often, and I was pretty all-in on the whole thing.

When VW introduced the new Atlas in 2018, it marked the automaker’s first dedicated attempt at a midsize three-row crossover built specifically for the North American market. The Atlas offered a huge amount of interior room, a smooth, car-like ride quality, and signature Volkswagen features. along with a plain dashboard and seats with middling comfort.
This second-generation Atlas brings new styling, a much more upscale interior, improved seating, updated technology, and a modest increase in engine output. Volkswagen has also made some improvements to the driving experience and fuel economy. Those are worthwhile changes, even if they don't exactly qualify as splashy headlines.
After all, when a vehicle's primary mission is transporting children, sports equipment, and adults who have opinions about second-row legroom, excitement isn't necessarily the highest priority. The question is whether the new Atlas makes everyday family life easier without introducing a fresh collection of things to complain about.
The answer is mostly yes, especially now that the highest trim level brings some serious Harman Kardon punch.

The 2027 Atlas wears entirely new sheet metal, with an updated design that maintains the upright proportions and squared-off appearance that helped make the original popular. The styling is more contemporary, featuring revised lighting, a bolder front end, and a more sculpted appearance.
It remains recognizably an Atlas, which is probably a good thing. Volkswagen hasn't attempted to turn its three-row family hauler into a rolling origami experiment or a study in aerodynamic sculpture. The proportions remain practical, and the overall design avoids some of the visual excesses found elsewhere in the segment. Whether the result handsome or simply inoffensive is a matter of taste.
More importantly, Volkswagen hasn't abandoned the Atlas' fundamental mission. This is still a midsize SUV designed around passenger accommodation rather than the pursuit of sporty driving dynamics or a rugged image that will never see anything rougher than a gravel driveway.
That focus carries over into the cabin, where the changes are most meaningful. The outgoing Atlas was already spacious, but the 2027 model makes a more concerted effort to deliver the sort of interior buyers expect when spending north of US$40,000 on a family vehicle.
Materials have improved, and the redesigned cabin features a cleaner layout, upgraded trim, and a premium atmosphere. Depending on trim, buyers can get features including Nappa leather upholstery, heated and ventilated front seats, ambient lighting, and an upgraded audio system that jumps from ""good"" to ""concert hall quality.""
The seating arrangement remains one of the vehicle's strongest selling points. There's generous room in all three rows, making it a convincing option for families who regularly carry four or more people. While many competitors technically offer three rows, their third-row seating seems designed primarily for emergency use. The Atlas carries full-sized passengers comfortably in the back.
Cargo flexibility is another strength, and the roomy cabin provides useful storage throughout. Volkswagen has also reworked the center console, relocating the gear selector to the steering column to free up additional space for storage and wireless charging equipment. It’s a practical layout shift that makes sense in a vehicle where every compartment quickly becomes home to phones, water bottles, and snack wrappers.

The 2027 Atlas features a larger central infotainment display, measuring 12.9 inches in the base SE and 15 inches in higher trims. The screens are bright, responsive, and modern, while a 10.3-inch digital instrument display provides essential driving information. Wireless Apple CarPlay and Android Auto are standard, alongside multiple USB-C charging ports and wireless phone charging.
However, many climate functions remain integrated into the touchscreen interface. Adjusting the temperature shouldn't require the driver to navigate digital menus while keeping an eye on traffic.
Volkswagen has made welcome progress by restoring physical steering-wheel controls and adding a center-console dial that handles functions like volume and drive modes. Still, simple temperature knobs would remain a superior solution for distraction-free operation while driving. A touchscreen is an excellent place for navigation maps – it's a less compelling substitute for a simple temperature knob.

Under the hood, the 2027 Atlas uses an updated turbocharged 2.0-liter four-cylinder engine producing 282 horsepower (207 kW) and 258 lb-ft (350 Nm) of torque. That's an increase of 13 horsepower (9.6 kW) over the outgoing model’s 2.0L TSI and about equivalent to the 3.6L V6 that was discontinued in 2023. To be frank, most of this new engine power isn’t very noticeable.
An eight-speed automatic transmission handles shifting, with front-wheel drive standard and all-wheel drive available. The engine is perfectly adequate for daily driving, providing enough power to merge onto highways, navigate urban traffic, and haul the family toward its next destination. But adequate is not the same as enthusiastic. Most of the added HP is lost due to the slightly heavier weight of the 2027 model versus the outgoing 2026. About 148 pounds (67 kg) if you average the trim levels.
In other words, the Atlas isn't going to make you reconsider your life choices because you missed an opportunity to buy a sports sedan. Nor should it. This is a family SUV.
Fuel economy is more encouraging. Front-wheel-drive models are rated at 22 mpg in the city, 29 mpg on the highway, and 25 mpg combined (10.7 / 8.1 / 9.4 l/100km). All-wheel-drive versions drop about two points from each of those numbers.
Those figures represent modest improvements over comparable versions of the previous Atlas. Unfortunately, Volkswagen still doesn't offer a hybrid powertrain. That's becoming an increasingly significant omission in a segment where competitors are finding ways to combine substantial passenger space with considerably better fuel economy.
A hybrid Atlas is planned for a future update, but buyers shopping today will have to make do with straight gasoline power.

Outside of the interior upgrades, the biggest improvement in the new Atlas is its drive dynamics. Volkswagen has revised the suspension and steering to improve its road manners. The result is a vehicle that feels more controlled than its predecessor, with less of the loose, floaty behavior that could make the previous model feel heavy on uneven roads.
The steering is improved, too, helping the Atlas feel more predictable through corners. It's easier to place on the road, and the overall experience is less tiring during ordinary driving. There is still a bit of an unsettled feeling when driving over broken pavement, with sharp impacts occasionally making their way into the cabin. The Atlas remains comfortable enough on smoother highways, but rough surfaces expose some of its limitations.
This isn't a disaster. Most owners won't spend their weekends conducting instrumented suspension evaluations on poorly maintained rural roads. They'll be driving to work, taking the children to school, and occasionally embarking on long-distance family trips. For those purposes, the Atlas is generally competent and easy to live with.
The Atlas can tow up to 5,000 pounds (2,268 kg) when properly equipped, giving it useful capability for small campers, boats, and recreational trailers.

The biggest challenge facing the 2027 Atlas isn't the previous Atlas, it's the increasingly capable competition. The midsize three-row SUV segment includes the Honda Pilot, Hyundai Palisade, Kia Telluride, and Toyota Grand Highlander. Each brings its own combination of space, technology, comfort, and powertrain options.
The redesigned Atlas starts at US$43,135, including its base SE configuration, while a fully equipped SEL Premium R-Line reaches $58,135. These prices include destination charges of $1,525.
That's competitive territory, but it also puts the Atlas in a position where buyers have plenty of alternatives to investigate before signing paperwork.
Volkswagen's improvements to cabin materials and passenger accommodation help strengthen its case. Its spacious third row remains a particularly compelling advantage for families who regularly transport several adults or older children. However, the lack of a hybrid option, the touchscreen-heavy climate controls, and the occasionally unsettled ride prevent the Atlas from separating itself from the pack.
None of those issues makes the 2027 Atlas a bad choice. They simply make it a vehicle that requires shoppers to consider what matters most to them. For families prioritizing space, practical features, and a more premium cabin without moving into a full-size SUV, this New Atlas writer thinks the new Atlas deserves a place on the shopping list.
Product Page: 2027 Volkswagen Atlas

87,2026-10-24,"Gen Z investors are scrambling to protect their portfolios from political uncertainty as the midterms approach, but their anxiety could end up costing them.
A whopping 72% of Gen Z investors said they had already made changes to their retirement savings or investment portfolios in anticipation of the November elections, according to a survey from the Nationwide Retirement Institute. That’s far more than the 17% of Baby Boomers who reported doing the same.
But the gap isn’t a stampede for the exits. About as many Gen Z investors added money to take advantage of current conditions as moved into safer investments, according to the survey. The first generation to grow up with free trades on their phones is treating the election as a market event. 
Boomers’ decision to wait it out may be less about hard-won wisdom than about where they are in life. Most boomers are drawing down their savings or close to it, so a portfolio “change” for them usually means selling. They’re also more likely to already have an adviser — 43% do, according to Northwestern Mutual — and for shares held in taxable accounts, holding until death lets heirs skip the capital-gains bill entirely.
Across all generations, 40% of investors have made changes ahead of the election on Nov. 3, which will determine which party controls either chamber of Congress. 
Mark Hackett, the chief market strategist for Nationwide’s Investment Management Group, told Fortune that younger investors aren’t necessarily more fearful than older generations. Instead, they are changing the way they interact with financial markets as well as how they react to the massive amounts of financial information and trading tools at their fingertips.
“Younger investors came of age in an environment where investing is highly mobile, social and increasingly real-time, so adjusting a portfolio can feel less like a major decision and more like an ongoing process with almost no friction between a headline, an investing app and a potential portfolio adjustment,” Hackett said.
That accessibility can be both good and bad. While younger investors have unprecedented access to financial information, it also means they have more opportunities to make impulsive decisions based on breaking news instead of a company’s financials or an analysis of economic conditions.
“The question, therefore, is less about risk tolerance or volatility, and more about whether greater access to information is improving decision-making or simply increasing the temptation to trade,” Hackett said. “The latter can be fatal for portfolio longevity.”
“For younger investors, frequent portfolio changes can interrupt the power of compounding over decades,” he said.
The survey, conducted between late August and early September, included 2,029 American investors with at least $10,000 in investable assets and 538 financial advisers and financial professionals.
Despite the potential for turbulence caused by the midterms, the financial advisers surveyed recommend staying the course. More than half said maintaining an existing investment strategy is generally the best approach during an election year. 
The market’s previous performance helps explain their advice.
Since 1950 the S&P 500 has consistently risen in the 12 months following midterm elections and has returned gains of 15% on average, according to J.P. Morgan Asset Management. While the past doesn’t predict future performance, selling in times of uncertainty can be a gamble.
Hackett said he recommends investors maintain a diversified portfolio and rebalance their investments only periodically depending on their desired risk exposure.
That doesn’t mean investors should never make changes. Hackett said adjusting investments to reflect personal financial goals, changes in risk tolerance, or shifting income needs are good reasons to make changes. Still, they should exercise caution, he added.
“Investors should be careful not to mistake a source of uncertainty for a source of lasting impairment to long-term returns,” he said.
Role: Reporter
Marco Quiroz-Gutierrez is a reporter for Fortune covering general business news.

88,2026-10-24,"Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality
Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.
TL;DR: Franklin Templeton has filed exchange registrations for two stock-and-Bitcoin ETFs. Their strategy redirects equity dividends into crypto exposure, with portfolio rules designed to keep Bitcoin a minority allocation.
Franklin Templeton is moving closer to offering investors a way to combine a US stock portfolio with an automatic Bitcoin allocation. The idea uses dividend reinvestment to build crypto exposure, while periodically trimming it back.
An October 22 Form 8-A registers shares of the Franklin US Equity Bitcoin DRIP Index ETF and Franklin US Innovation Bitcoin DRIP Index ETF for Nasdaq. The filing is an exchange-registration step; it does not establish that either fund has begun trading.
For investors, the appeal would be operational simplicity. One fund could handle both the equity portfolio and the rules governing its smaller Bitcoin component, avoiding a separate decision each time a dividend arrives.
The accompanying prospectus filing proposes an October 27 effective date. It gives the equity version the ticker FBEQ and the innovation version FIBT, with annual management fees of 0.09% and 0.19%, respectively.
The VettaFi indexes started with 95% equities and 5% Bitcoin. Their dividend mechanism reinvests regular and special equity dividends into Bitcoin at the following day’s open after the ex-dividend date.
At quarterly rebalancing, a Bitcoin weighting above 5% is reduced to 4.5%. A separate breach above 20% between reviews triggers a reset to 4.5% at the close of the second following business day. That is a corrective rule, not a guarantee the allocation can never temporarily exceed 20%.
The funds can use Bitcoin-linked investment products and derivatives to reproduce the index exposure. A shareholder would own ETF shares, rather than receive coins in a personal wallet.
Economically, this makes the reinvestment decision part of the product. Dividends are still money belonging to the portfolio. Redirecting them into another asset does not create an extra return by itself, and a lower entry cost for that asset does not guarantee a profitable exit.
Franklin already offers a more concentrated route through its Bitcoin ETF, EZBC. The issuer describes that product as holding Bitcoin and cash, making its value closely dependent on the coin’s market performance.
The proposed DRIP funds would instead leave most of the investment tied to equities. That means stock selection and the behaviour of the chosen benchmark could matter more to the overall return than a modest move in Bitcoin.
The wrapper also changes the practical meaning of custody. Investors trade fund shares through an exchange; they are not managing the keys or selecting each Bitcoin-related instrument themselves.
The next milestone is the funds becoming effective and opening for trading. Until then, the filings describe a planned allocation strategy, with fees and safeguards to examine, rather than a live track record showing how it performs.
Written by the News Desk and edited by Samuel Rae.

89,2026-10-24,"Author: Jake Bauer | Published: 2021-11-08 | Updated: 2026-08-21
Based on certain kinds of feedback I've received in
response to this post (which says a lot about the current state of Free
Software culture), I feel the need to say that if you disagree with what I have
written, I would appreciate it if you would voice those disagreements by
criticising the ideas that I've presented rather than assuming things about my
character or level of experience and insulting me. I have no problem with
people disagreeing with me; I appreciate having discussions in good faith, and
hope that you would give me the benefit of the doubt. You might even be
surprised at what I actually believe.
In this post, I use the term ""Free Software"" to
mean software licensed with Copyleft licenses, and to explicitly
differentiate it from permissively-licensed ""Open Source"" software
(which is what the Free Software Foundation and Co. also do).
I used to be a staunch software freedom evangelist. I used to license
all my works GPLv3-or-later and CC-BY-SA wherever I could, and I used to
believe strongly in the words of Richard Stallman. I have since started
to think more about the real effects of the Free Software movement and
have changed the way I write and license my software as a result.
Although I believe FOSS as a concept is still very important, I simply
no longer believe in the Free Software movement.
I’ve come to the conclusion that Free Software is an abject failure.
While Free Software may on its face sound like a good conceptâespecially
with the kind of language often used to describe the movement and its
opponentsâwhen put under scrutiny, the institutions and practices that
make up the Free Software movement fundamentally fail at their stated
goals. The Free Software movement is doing more harm to FOSS than good.
Not only is it an ideological mess, it hampers collaboration, it is
legally ineffective, it makes the lives of developers harder while doing
nothing actually useful for users, and it fundamentally gets in the way
of a thriving, free, and open software ecosystem.
Why Software Should Be Free by Richard Stallman
presents an argument against having owners of software and explains the harm
done by obstructing software development through proprietary licensing. He
posits that software with obstructions results in fewer users, the inability
for users to fix programs, and the inability of developers to build upon prior
knowledge or work. He also states that the justifications one uses for keeping
ownership of the software (which he equates with keeping the software
proprietary) are emotional (i.e. “This software is mine, and I wish to control
it”) and economical (i.e. “I wish to become wealthy by programming.”). He shuns
these excuses and spends the rest of the document refuting those excuses, and
explaining why the existence of proprietary software is bad and why the
alternativeâsoftware not having ownersâis better. By and large, I agree with
this essay: I don’t think software should have owners. Instead, I believe
software should be released into the public domain as much as is possible.
However, in nearly every Free Software project which exists today, there is a
clear “owner” of the softwareâsomeone who is the copyright holder, benevolent
dictator for life, or simply de facto leader of the project and through whom
all contributions must flow. Whether we’re talking about large projects that
are too large to fork and maintain by anybody but large, well-funded groups
such as the Linux kernel (in which Linus still has complete veto power by the
way), Qt (in which the company controls the development of the software and
simply allows older versions to be used under a free license), Android
(where Google maintains pretty solid control), or smaller projects which have
simple leaders. As long as there is a copyright statement, there are one or
more owners to be aware of.
Additionally, the GNU Manifesto, aside from calling
the Open Source movement an “amoral approach”, goes on to say that GNU “is not
in the public domain” and will have restrictions placed on further
modifications in the form of disallowing proprietary modifications, with the
justification given by Stallman being: “I want to make sure that all versions
of GNU remain free.” With that, Stallman does exactly what he shunned in Why
Software Should Be Free. He uses his emotional attachment to the software,
something he said was an excuse people use to defend why software has an owner,
to justify his want to control how others can distribute and modify his code.
He is effectively saying “this is my creation, and I wish to control what
others can do with it” while also clearly assigning an owner to the software.
Update 2021-12-13: I was recently made aware of an article in which Richard
 Stallman advocates against positive changes to copyright laws because of how
 it would hurt Free Software. Instead of making software free of copyright
 after 5 years, he advocates for maintaining copyright control because it would
 hurt the control that Free Software licenses would have over the distribution
 of software later on. It’s not enough that the software would still fulfill
 the four pillars of free software after the 5 years is up, control must be
 maintained over how other people can distribute and use the software in their
 products in case they ever decide to fork it and make something proprietary
 from it. Not only does he not trust that people would use only the Free
 version (as if we need to be protected by Free Software licenses lest we fall
 prey to encountering proprietary software), he is, in essence, advocating for
 maintaining ownership over software even though the change to copyright
 legislation would be positive for society overall. The article is called: How the Swedish Pirate
 Party Platform Backfires on Free Software.
Even if you’re also staunchly against proprietary software, you should at least
recognize the hypocrisy in this stance.
If software is not in the public domain, then, by definition, it has to have at
least one owner. If Stallman can dictate how others can use his softwareâeven
if it is more free than most proprietary softwareâthen it clearly has an
owner.
Simply put, Stallman and others in the Free Software community use the exact
same excuses that he criticized in Why Software Should Be Free as
justification for their actions. GNU, the GPL, and seemingly the entirety of
Free Software as it stands today are all based on the same premises as
proprietary software. They use existing copyright systems in what they deem
the “right” way while simultaneously criticizing the way others use them as
wrong.
The Free Software movement comes off as both a “cult of
personality""âworshipping Richard Stallman and his
teachings while ignoring
the inconsistencies between his words and his actionsâas well as a “cult
of ideology""âshunning those who disagree with the manifestos and the
“way of life” that the movement espouses to an extreme degree. As long as you,
 as a developer, do everything within the framework of the GPL and the culture
 of Free Software, you are considered ethical and good. As soon as you wish to
 do something outside of this domainâeven by using a more open, permissive
 licenseâyou are considered amoral.
In an article where Linus Torvalds criticizes the
GPLv3,
Torvalds even says:
“I think the GPLv3 is expressly designed to not allow [the meeting between
open source and free software people]. Exactly because the FSF considers us
open source people ‘heretics.’”
One of the stated goals written in the GNU Manifesto is:
“Finally, the overhead of considering who owns the system software and what
one is or is not entitled to do with it will be lifted.”
One look at the GPL or any other Free Software license will tell you that they
have utterly failed at this. For example, the GPL has become so complicated
that only a programmer with relatively advanced legal knowledge and ability to
read and properly understand “legalese” will be able to decipher it to know
what they are able and unable to do with it. There exist websites which explain
the license in plain English, but even those say their explanations are no
substitute for reading the license. There are very specific meanings that many
of the words in the license take on when put in the context of our modern legal
system so, while you may be able to read the GPL and think that you
understand it, unless you are well-educated in this legal domain, you probably
don’t fully understand the true meaning and effect that the words of the
license would actually have in court.
Especially because the ramifications of the GPL are still not even fully
understood by lawyers themselves. Many large companies will shy away from the
GPL simply because they don’t want to take the risk of using GPL-licensed code
improperly and being forced to reveal their proprietary code. While I don’t
agree with proprietary software as a concept, the fact that even lawyersâwho
are supposed to be expertly trained in the kind of language used in the
licenseâare uneasy about its terms further reinforces just how unapproachable
it is to the everyday software developer. GNU and the GPL have done nothing to
remove the overhead of considering who owns software and how it can be used.
Update 2023-07-13: It has become especially clear how few people
actually
understand
what the GPL does and does not permit based on the responses to changes
that Red Hat made to how they offer their source code (more on this later).
Since the development of the GPL is reactionaryâthat is to say, its development
and growth over time was in response to discovered workaroundsâthere is now
even more overhead by way of the extra complication of the “-or-later/only”
clauses. So this is now a license that has multiple versions which are not
backwards compatible. A project licensed under the GPLv2-only cannot integrate
GPLv3-or-later code without being re-licensed as GPLv3. The Linux kernel is an
excellent example of this.
Linus Torvalds even came out strongly against the GPLv3
and the process by which the FSF created the license. Many others in the
industry also saw this as an extreme and unnecessary move by the FSF to wield
the ultimate power over the GPLv3; all because a manufacturer put Linux in
their products and blocked users from running their own modified software on
that hardware. The absurdity of this line of thinking is compounded by the fact
that this had absolutely nothing to do with the Linux kernel since it’s the
system’s firmware that would stop users from running different software. This
seems to many as just an excuse to expand the scope and powers of the GPL over
something completely out of the scope of the GPL-licensed software. The move by
the FSF, the actions of Stallman, and the “tivoization” rhetoric are even
heavily criticized by Software Freedom Conservancy member Bradley M. Kuhn.
Putting aside the political manoeuvrings of the FSF, the mere existence
of an “-or-later” clause is a ridiculous thing to attach to a license.
Anybody who licenses their project under a GPLv3-or-later license puts
a lot of trust in the stewards of the GPL that the next version of the
GPL will align with their values and goals; a GPLv3-or-later project
will be able to be licensed under a GPLv4 license, whatever the clauses
of that GPLv4 license. This is a lot of stock to put into a group of
people like the FSF who were so dogmatic and exclusionary in their
development of the GPLv3.
The reality of the GPL is that there is still a lot of overhead in
considering who owns the software and how it can be used. Not only in
whether or not a developer should choose version 2 or version 3 of the
license depending on their goals, but also for developers and users
alike who try to understand the language of the license to determine
what they can do with the software and, if they are integrating some
GPL-licensed code into their own product, who actually owns and has
copyright over that software.
Those who wish to integrate GPL-licensed code into their otherwise
non-GPL-licensed projects are faced with the decision to relicense their code
under the GPL, remake the functionality of the library under a more open
license, or otherwise abandon those efforts altogether. While the GPL
may “prevent” corporations or people from taking GPL-licensed code and
integrating it into a proprietary product, it also prevents many other
non-GPL-licensed projects from using GPL-licensed code, even other FOSS
projects.
GPL fans said the great problem we would face is that companies would
take our BSD code, modify it, and not give back.  Nope – the great
problem we face is that people would wrap the GPL around our code, and
lock us out in the same way that these supposed companies would lock us
out.  Just like the Linux community, we have many companies giving us
code back, all the time.  But once the code is GPL’d, we cannot get it
back.
– Theo DeRaadt, Message to the Linux Kernel Mailing List
Even so, it’s not like the GPL actually prevents corporations from
stealing GPL-licensed code and integrating it into projects. While there
are plenty of corporations who freely comply with the GPL, there are
plenty more, such as VMWare, who don’t comply and yet don’t face
consequences.
Even if a lawsuit is successful, it is usually at the cost of members
of the FOSS community who burn out or are
left disenchanted by the whole process. Being GPL-licensed also doesn’t
prevent a project from being bought and therefore having all control
handed over to a corporation. This was most recently seen in the
acquisition of Audacity by Muse
Group
in which Muse Group bought the rights to the Audacity code and project.
Users have since forked the project1, but this doesn’t necessarily stop
something like this from happening in the future, and certainly doesn’t
stop it from happening to other projects. If software actually didn’t
have owners, this could not happen.
Update 2022-10-23: It just happened to
Gitea!
Update 2023-07-16: Being under the GPL also doesn’t even stop projects
from being closed-source except to paying customers. As just happened
with Red Hat Enterprise
Linux!
Red Hat is making Red Hat Enterprise Linux’s source code available only
to paying customers. Since there’s no other way to get a copy from Red
Hat (you can’t just download it like you can Ubuntu), this is entirely
within the letter of the GPLv2. They’ve also said they’ll terminate
support contracts and not give customers new versions of the code if
those customers exercise their rights under the GPL by distributing the
code they receive from Red Hat. This angered a huge portion of the FOSS
community who shouted from the rooftops about how this violates the
“spirit” of Free Software. However, this is nothing new, and a company
was doing the same thing in 2006 to
which the Free Software Foundation said “that’s ok!”. That “spirit”
clearly doesn’t matter when it comes to a company’s bottom line and
neither the GPL nor the FSF do anything to address this.
The GPL-family of licenses attempt to solve a societal problem by
restricting the distribution of software in a manner not unlike the
so-called Ethical
Software
movement. The GPL acts effectively as a proprietary license that allows
you to do the things it deems ethical, so long as you abide by the terms
of the license with regards to the distribution and re-licensing
restrictions. As long as you are in this “club”, everything is fine and
dandy and you can make your changes and push them to your favourite
projects. As soon as you leave this club and want to pull code from
a GPL-licensed project into your MIT-licensed project, well, sorry, too
bad for you.
Update 2026-08-21: Even more onerous is the AGPL, which stops even
GPL-licensed software from including code without infecting the whole project
with its terms. An instance of this being an issue for a popular GPL project
just happened with the code forge software Forgejo, where
they had to stop using an image library because it turned out to be
AGPL-licensed. This would have required Forgejo to be distributed under the
terms of the AGPL, rather than their preferred Free Software license, the GPL.
This is FOSS friendly-fire at its best. Congratulations, you played
yourselves.
While the GPL may not cause as much of an obstruction or be as unethical
as proprietary software, it is frankly not that much better. It causes
distress and conflict in the software community, is difficult for
non-lawyers to fully understand, and harms developers of
non-GPL-licensed FOSS software all while not even being effective at
what it was ostensibly designed to do. It is, for example, the reason
why the BSDs cannot take improvements made in the Linux kernel and
directly integrate them into their own kernels, and it is the reason why
ZFS cannot be integrated into the Linux
kernel,
but can be shipped with FreeBSD.
For all this talk of Free Software being the ethical option and the GPL
being the ultimate defender of user and developer rights, it completely
goes against the ethical principles laid out in Why Software Should Be
Free.
The GNU Manifesto speaks a lot about how:
“Users will no longer be at the mercy of one programmer or company which owns
the sources and is in sole position to make changes.”
But a cursory look at the current landscape of Free Software will tell
you that this is simply not the case at all.
Users are absolutely at the mercy of the maintainers of software projects to
integrate their changes in the current landscape of software development,
especially with software projects as complicated as many of the popular GUI
toolkits, kernels, or desktop environments. In fact, the reality is that Free
Software project issue boards and forums are not much better than corporate
support forums. Often when one opens an issue in a large software project it
can take months or even years for those issues to be worked out. It’s also not
uncommon for those issues to simply go unsolved for many years. A great example
of this is the ‘Add an “icon view with thumbnails” mode’ GNOME issue
which was opened in 2004 (it’s almost old enough to vote!) and is still
not actually fixed despite many users expressing how useful it would be to have
this featureapparently has been recently added nearly 20 years
later. Good job everyone!
Users simply cannot maintain such complex software on their own and, if they
have limited programming ability, cannot be expected to fork a project, make
their fix, test their fix, act on feedback from the maintainers (if they’re
lucky enough to get their patch or pull request noticed), and so on. The
current software development ecosystem makes this impossible, regardless of the
license of the project. Software is not simple enough for that and GNU and Free
Software did nothing to solve this. In fact, it can be argued that they made
this worse by contributing to the creation of a culture of “elitists” who
expect all the users of their software to have the ability to read code,
technical discussions, and documentation in order to understand and fix
problems.
If an experienced user does come along and is dis-satisfied with their
corporate support forum-like experience, they might be inclined to fork
the project. However another very common sight in the FOSS realm is to
see accusations towards groups or companies who fork projects of making
“hostile forks” or committing “theft” of Free Software. For example,
Members of the Free Software community shunned and criticized the group
of developers who forked Gogs to make
Gitea
when they forked a Free Software project to extend it for their own
needs because of an unresponsive maintainer. They even still kept the
project under the same license (an “unethical” Open Source license, no
less).
Update 2022-09-13: I recently learned of the XFree86 fiasco in which
another, similar incident occurred where one of the original developers
of the X window system felt that the current state of things was
untenable and that the project needed to be forked and replaced. He was
summarily shunned and kicked from the
project
for his actions. This is yet another example of how forking is viewed by
many who work in FOSS and who wish to maintain control over their
particular piece of software.
If software is not supposed to have an owner, as Why Software Should Be
Free advocates, what exactly is being stolen? Why do they, as the
creator of some piece of software, care if some company or even another
FOSS developer takes it and uses it in their product or makes
proprietary modifications with it?2 Why Software Should
Be Free specifically outs this need to control what happens with one’s
software as one of the main justifications for proprietary licensing and
something to be avoided, yet it is seen everywhere in the Free Software
culture. In fact, an article entitled Don’t fear the fork: How DVCS
aids open source
development
perfectly exemplifies how the Free Software culture views forks (of
course, it’s written from the perspective of the “amoral” Open Source
movement).
The goal of Free Software as laid out by Why Software Should Be Free
was to create a world in which there were no owners of software and in
which distribution was not restricted. Yet, despite this, the way the
GPL family of licenses puts restrictions on the distribution of
GPL-licensed software combined with the culture of Free Software
actively encourage both of those things and actively discourage
forking software and modifying it to suit your own needs. From the point
of view of Why Software Should Be Free, the GPL combined with this
culture is, in reality, not much better than the world of proprietary
software we had before as far as users (and developers to a lesser
extent) are concerned.
The degree of openness which exists in the current software landscape
can be, to a large degree, attributed instead to the Open Source
movement3. It happened in spite of the Free Software movement, not
because of it.
Free Software and the surrounding culture and institutions are often
viewed as a farce outside of the dedicated Free Software cult
community. The FSF has been slowly descending into irrelevancy for the
past two decades as they struggle to do anything meaningful with their
time and resources; instead opting to make a lot of unproductive noise.
Take, as recent examples, how they mailed a hard drive to Microsoft
telling them to put the Windows 7 source code on
it,
or how they accuse Apple of “censoring free
software”
âdespite the fact that Apple isn’t actually censoring anything and
plenty of Free Software projects such as
Telegram are on the App
Storeâand
also criticizing them for not supporting their specific chosen free
media codecsâonce again, ignoring the plenty of other free media codecs
which are otherwise perfectly supported.
To expand on that last point about Free Software projects on the App
Store, the Apple App Store
EULA
contains restrictions that the GPL doesn’t allow (see section 10 of the
GPLv3 and section 6 of the GPLv2). It says:
Except as provided in the Usage Rules, you may not distribute or make the
Licensed Application available over a network where it could be used by
multiple devices at the same time. You may not transfer, redistribute or
sublicense the Licensed Application […] You may not copy (except as
permitted by this license and the Usage Rules), reverse-engineer, disassemble,
attempt to derive the source code of, modify, or create derivative works of
the Licensed Application, any updates, or any part thereof…
Note that this only applies to the application you purchased in the App store,
as bundled by the App Store. It doesn’t prevent you from going to GitHub and
modifying the software there, it only prevents you from legally modifying the
software you got from the Apple App Store. Sure, this isn’t a good thing, yet
it is hardly “censoring free software” given that, as the owner of a Free
Software project, you can freely submit GPL-licensed software to the App Store
and Apple assumes you have the rights to provide them with a non-GPL-licensed
build.
If you don’t have that ability because you don’t own exclusive rights to the
software and it’s not feasible for you to get the permission of every
contributor with their name attached to the project, well, that’s not Apple’s
problem. The FSF words their article like Apple is attacking them when, in
reality, Apple doesn’t care what license you’ve chosen, only that you grant
them the ability to distribute your app under the terms of their EULA. The
wording in that blog post is yet another example of why the FSF are seen as a
farce. They should know better.
This reputation of preferring ideological book-thumping over meaningful action
is nothing new. Most who are familiar with Linux are likely familiar with
Richard Stallman’s “GNU+Linux” rant
in which he squabbles and nitpicks over the relevancy of GNU to the Linux name.
Ignoring that the only significant contributions to the Linux system GNU can
claim is the compiler, the coreutils, and the C library. Significant? Yes, but
this ignores that most Linux systems today run far more non-GNU software than
GNU software.
In fact, even those GNU components are quickly fading into irrelevancy as
projects like Clang/LLVM, rewrites of coreutils to be faster and cleaner
(e.g. Busybox or various Rust projects), and other libc’s such as muslc are
gaining more and more traction with each passing day. GNU is simply failing to
make any meaningful and actual progress, and is grasping at straws in an
attempt to remain relevant. Not only have other projects such as OpenBSD long
since abandoned things like modern GCC due to later versions becoming
unmaintainable, buggy behemoths, GNU themselves haven’t even released their
HURD kernel in any meaningful capacity despite promising to create such a thing
since before Linux existed.
Speaking of fading into irrelevancy, many of the Free Software institutions such
as the Software Freedom Conservancy and Free Software Foundation Europe have
either been reliant on GPL violation lawsuits as one of their primary means to
exist, or have been relatively ineffective at carrying out their mandates with
regards to litigation. It has gotten so bad that the SFC have tried to bring
lawsuits on behalf of the users of GPL software; something which has little basis in actual
copyright law, but is the only option they have left when companies either
skillfully hide their GPL violations or the owners of GPL software are
unwilling to enforce the license against large opponents. A cursory glance on
the SFC’s website reveals a lot of information about this Vizio lawsuit, but
not much information about previous lawsuits or successes. No wonder these
institutions also tend to only go after small opponents. Even very public
violations such as those committed by OnePlus
have still gone unanswered for. If such violations are clearly allowed to
continue with such ineffective enforcement, what even is the use of the GPL in
reality?
Regarding the quality of GNU software, outside of the Free Software
community, GNU code has a reputation for being resource-hungry, buggy,
bloated, or annoying and frustrating to work with, as many who have had
the pleasure of using alternative libc’s, compilers, utilities, or
programs will attest. GNU creations have a reputation for being
needlessly complexâsolving problems which don’t really exist in the
first place if you write good software (or documentation)âand having
bafflingly complex source code. The GNU Info system and the code found
in many of the coreutils are good examples of this.
Finally, we can talk about how the conduct of Richard Stallman himself
reflects on the whole Free Software movement. The Free Software movement
went through its own little “constitutional crisis” over the question of
whether or not to keep him on as the face of Free Software after he made
some comments on an MIT mailing list about a sensitive topic. I won’t go
into specifics about that situation here, since it has been covered to
death in other media outlets and can be easily searched for (search
“stallman mit mailing list”), but, suffice to say, Stallman acted in
a completely improper and inept manner and, in fact, that is far from
the only example of him behaving in such a way.
In 2007, Stallman sent a message to the OpenBSD-misc mailing
list entitled
“Real men don’t attack straw men” in which he accuses the OpenBSD folk
of making straw-man arguments about himself, and then using those
arguments to attack his credibility. If you read through this mailing
list thread, you don’t have to go far to realize that Stallman has
absolutely no clue what he’s talking about. He bases his opinion of
OpenBSD on “what I have heard” and presumes that the OpenBSD folk care
whether or not he recommends their system. He proceeds to have
everything he said get taken apart by the replies which point out all
the inconsistencies in his own arguments and stances.
After seeing just how ineffective this culture is at advocating for and
enforcing its principles in a meaningful way, as well as seeing the conduct
of one of its top-most members (not to mention the conduct from its
supporters),
how could anyone view this as a movement to be appreciated, let alone joined
and celebrated?
I think, by now, it is accurate to say that Free Software is an abject
failure.
It has failed at its stated goals through both the licenses and the
culture it has created and has instead perpetuated the paradigm of
software ownership and the use of unethical and flawed copyright
legislation. It has done very little to prevent corporate takeover of
projects, improve the software development landscape, or make the lives
of developers or users easier. It has done nothing that more ethical,
permissive software licenses and the culture of collaboration born out
of projects such as BSD Unix didn’t already do, except to create a toxic
subculture of Free Software cult worshippers.
One cannot solve the copyright, ownership, or restricted distribution
problems of software through playing into the exact same flawed systems
that allow such problems to exist in the first place. These are problems
which must be solved on a societal level, not on the level of individual
software projects through obtuse licensing. Free Software, as it stands
today, has the very real effect of restricting innovation, sharing, and
collaboration between developers.
None of this is to say that proprietary licenses are okay or even that
the Open Source movement is the alternative. In fact, the Open Source
Initiative is not all that much better than the Free Software
Foundation, it just has a mostly different set of problems which are out
of scope for this essay.
For the most part, I agree with Why Software Should Be Free. Stallman
has perfectly valid and good ideas in that essay, yet he misses the mark
with their implementation. He’s right in that software should not have
owners and restricting software distribution is unethical; I ultimately
believe that nobody should get to control how their software is used,
studied, distributed, or hacked upon. Software should be as much in the
public domain as possible just like books, music, product schematics
and other such things should be.
The only licenses which truly meet all these criteria under that framework are
public domain-equivalent licenses. Licensing your code under the Unlicense, the 0BSD License, or the CC0 License means that you
do not place any restrictions on the software whatsoever. People don’t even
have to mention your name or your project when they use your code because you
disclaim any ownership of the software. The extremely popular SQLite uses this approach and has spread across
the entire industry as a result, to the benefit of everyone.
If you do at least wish to have your name attached to the code to claim credit,
commonly used permissive licenses are the best option. Licenses such as the
MIT License or the ISC License (preferred due to it being a
simplified MIT) impose no further restrictions other than maintaining a
copyright and permission notice for that code if it’s used in other projects.
Essentially, people are free to do whatever they want with your code, so long
as they keep your name on parts that you wrote.
Not only do these options provide far more real freedom4 to
developers5, they remove the overhead of having to think about
who owns the software. In the case of software in the public domain, you
don’t have to worry at all because there is no owner. In the case of
permissively-licensed software, it’s a simple matter of copy-pasting the
necessary notice(s) wherever that code is used. These licenses are also
relatively easy to understand (except perhaps the CC0), and the culture
surrounding them is generally such that you don’t have to worry about
a mob coming after you for forking a project and morphing it into
something that serves your needs, nor do you have to worry about being
sued for an accidental violation of a license that even people who are
well-versed in software licensing aren’t confident about getting right.
You’ll notice that those licenses, however, do not address the issue of
corporations controlling software development and distribution, people using
software in ways you may deem bad or harmful, or the existence of megasoftware.
This is because those are problems that cannot be solved or even effectively
addressed by software licensing. These are societal problems that must be
tackled on a societal level. Attempting to do otherwise harms and imposes more
restrictions on small teams, independent developers, and hobbyists who don’t
have the resources to ignore your restrictions or buy your development team.
In reality, by licensing your project under the GPL and participating in
Free Software culture, instead of limiting the harms that corporations
inflict, you end up limiting the good that the rest of us can do. Free
Software, as it stands today, has failed.
If this post intrigued you and made you want to
learn more, consider checking out:
You may also be interested in reading the discussion
on Lobste.rs.
It doesn’t seem to have gone anywhere or resulted in much, as is typical for these kinds of forks. While there is still commit activity in the repository, Tenacity isn’t even packaged for common Linux distributions like Debian or Fedora as of August, 2024, more than three years after the takeover of Audacity, has little-to-no userbase, and very little mindshare. It turns out that when maintainers of a project have financial backing, that project tends to be the one to succeed, regardless of how people may feel about it (which is why, for example, Forgejo, the fork of Gitea, seems to be doing quite well; it actually has financially-backed, dedicated developers). ↩
I actually completely understand not wanting a company to use your work for evil but, as we’ve already seen, copyleft licenses don’t stop this from happening. It’s not a problem that can be solved or even meaningfully addressed by a software license (I talk more about this in the conclusion). Not to mention that I’ve rarely seen this angle brought up in Free Software circles; the conversation usually focuses on monetary compensation. ↩
I have many problems with the current culture of the Open Source movement and their corporate-friendly centrism, but that’s an essay for another day. ↩
Some might see this as a “shallow understanding of freedom” which is a sentiment that, frankly, I find patronising and condescending. It’s not a “shallow understanding” of freedom, it’s a different interpretation of what freedom for software should look like, and a disagreement with the notion that it is good to foist rules and limitations onto other developers regarding how their software should be used and distributed under the guise of (a cumbersome and ultimately ineffective) guarantee of freedom. By real freedom I mean a lack of authority in the anarchist sense, not the libertarian sense. ↩
Many people have responded to this with “but the Free Software movement is about freedom for users, not developers!”. The only thing I can say to that without writing another essay is please go back and read the parts of the essay where I explain how the Free Software movement does nothing meaningful for users. No software licensing scheme, open or not, can empower users to exercise the freedoms they deserve to be able to exercise; we have to fundamentally change the way we write software to realize this goal. (Although, it can certainly be said that having a license that most people can actually understand is a valuable step towards realizing that goal.) ↩"

94,2026-10-24,"Help us verify your age by providing an honest response. This site contains gambling advertising for 24+.
You’re not old enough to view betting content. You’ll be redirected to the homepage.
The Rondo brings you a bit of everything: stories that matter, sharp insight, and the best and sometimes funniest of the beautiful game, delivered straight to your inbox every week.
Cesc Fabregas has reaffirmed his unwavering commitment to Como, dismissing any suggestions that he is looking for a way out of the Italian club. Despite his rising stock in the managerial world, the Spaniard insists his focus remains entirely on the project he has built at the Stadio Giuseppe Sinigaglia.
Fabregas has moved to end speculation regarding his future at Como, stating emphatically that he has never considered leaving the club he led to the top flight. The former Arsenal and Barcelona midfielder has seen his reputation as a coach skyrocket following a historic period for the Lariani, which recently included a stunning 4-1 victory over Manchester United in the Champions League. His success has naturally drawn admiring glances from across the continent.
Speaking at a press conference ahead of a Serie A clash with Sassuolo, Fabregas addressed questions about his longevity at the club and the possibility of a successor being lined up. ""I’ve never thought about leaving. If I started thinking about it, maybe that would be the end for me here, but I’ve never done it,"" the Spaniard explained, as quoted by Sky Sport Italia.
The focus on Fabregas's future was sparked by the upcoming meeting with Sassuolo boss Alberto Aquilani. The two former creative midfielders share a similar tactical philosophy, and Fabregas was quick to acknowledge the quality of his counterpart. ""I really like Alberto as a coach, for his ideas and his courage, and for how his teams play football,"" Fabregas remarked.
Fabregas revealed that their professional bond extended into the transfer market, specifically regarding the development of young talent. ""He called me last summer because he really wanted Rispoli at Catanzaro. Rispoli was the first player I ever had as a coach, so sending him to Alberto gave me complete peace of mind,"" he added.
Beyond his own future, Fabregas took time to discuss the meteoric rise of Nico Paz, who has become an indispensable figure in the Como starting XI. The young talent has consistently delivered high-level performances, making him one of the most talked-about prospects in Serie A. Fabregas explained that the player's psychological strength is just as impressive as his technical ability on the ball, allowing him to handle the pressures of elite football with ease.
25 Oct 202604:30
25 Oct 202610:00
25 Oct 202612:45
""You have to know the person and what kind of lad he is. Nico isn’t someone who needs to be helped along. The less you talk to him, the better. He has an incredible mentality,” Fabregas stated. He further emphasized the player's development by noting, ""We’ve been together for more than two years and he has grown enormously. He might even be among the team’s top three defenders. That mentality makes the difference before he even competes, and that’s why I never take him out.""
Ahead of the weekend fixture, Como are dealing with several significant absences in the attacking department. Fabregas confirmed that he will be without the services of Assane Diao and Moise Kean, both of whom are sidelined with fitness issues. The loss of Kean is particularly impactful given his recent form, though there is hope that the Italian international could make a swift return to the matchday squad for the club's next outing on Tuesday.
The manager also provided a pessimistic update on Martin Liberali, who is struggling with a minor physical complaint. While the injury is not considered serious, Fabregas admitted it was unlikely the player would be ready in time for the Sassuolo game."

98,2026-10-24,"India has reintroduced anti-dumping duties on titanium dioxide imports from China due to material injury caused to domestic producers. This action follows a recommendation from the Directorate General of Trade Remedies, which concluded that Chinese imports were sold at dumped prices. The duties will be effective for five years unless revoked or amended. This decision comes after the Calcutta High Court ordered a reconsideration of a previous duty case.
China faces fresh Indian duties on titanium dioxide imports
